## CHAPTER II.

### The Testimony of various illustrious Authors regarding this Secret <note>original: "Arcanum"; a deep secret or mystery, often used in alchemy to refer to the Philosopher's Stone or its effects.</note>.

First, Paracelsus <note>A famous Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of minerals and chemicals in medicine.</note> writes in *The Signature of Natural Things*, folio 358: "This is a true sign of the Philosophers' Tincture <note>original: "tincture of Philosophers"; another name for the Philosopher’s Stone or the substance used to transform metals.</note>: that by its power to transform, all imperfect metals are changed—namely, white metals into silver and red metals into the finest gold—if even the smallest portion of it is thrown into a crucible onto melted metal, and so on."

*Furthermore*, the invincible Star <note>original: "Astrum"; in alchemy, this refers to the inner celestial power or "virtue" of a substance.</note> of metals overcomes everything and changes them into a nature like its own. This gold and silver is nobler and better than that taken from metallic mines, and from it, superior medicinal secrets <note>original: "Arcana's"</note> can be prepared.

*Also*, every alchemist who possesses the Star of the Sun <note>The "Star of the Sun" likely refers to the solar or gold-producing essence of the Philosopher’s Stone.</note> can transform all red metals into gold, and so forth.

*Furthermore*, our Tincture of gold contains astral stars within it. It is an extremely stable <note>original: "fixt"; a substance that does not evaporate or change when exposed to heat.</note> substance and cannot be altered during the process of multiplication <note>The stage in alchemy where the power and quantity of the Philosopher’s Stone are increased.</note>. It is a powder that has a deep red... <meta>catchword: ...dest</meta>

It is transparent like a crystal, solid and very heavy, yet easily melted in fire like resin or wax before the flight of quicksilver <note>mercury</note>. Yet it flows without smoke, entering into solid bodies and penetrating them like oil through paper. It can be dissolved in and mixed with any liquid, is as brittle as glass, and can be ground into a powder like saffron. But in its whole mass, it shines red like a ruby (which redness is a sign of a perfect fixation <note>The process by which a volatile substance is rendered stable in fire.</note> and fixed perfection). It colors or stains permanently and remains stable through all tests and trials—even in the examination of burning sulfur itself and "devouring waters" <note>likely strong acids used to test metals, such as *aqua regia*</note>, and in the most intense heat of the fire. It is always incombustible and as permanent as a salamander <note>In alchemical lore, the salamander was a legendary creature that could live in and endure fire without being harmed.</note>, etc.

### Third, Van Helmont in *The Book of Eternal Life*, page 590. <note>original: "viz." (namely) and "Fol." (folio/page)</note>

I have often seen and handled the Stone, and I have performed a projection <note>The act of casting a small amount of the Philosophers' Stone onto base metal to transform it into gold or silver</note> using the fourth part of one grain wrapped in paper, cast upon eight ounces of quicksilver <note>mercury</note> boiling in a crucible. The quicksilver immediately stopped its movement <note>original: "Flux"</note> with a small noise and was congealed like yellow wax. After melting it with a bellows <note>original: "flux by blast"</note>, we found eight ounces—missing only eleven grains—of the purest gold. Therefore, one grain of this powder would transmute 19,186 parts of quicksilver into the best gold. 

Thus, this powder is found to share the same fundamental nature <note>original: "Similary parts"</note> as earthly things, and it transmutes an infinite abundance of impure metal into the best gold. It unites with the metal and protects it from corrosion <note>original: "Canker"</note>, rust, decay, and "death" <note>meaning chemical degradation</note>. It makes the metal nearly immortal against all the trials of fire and alchemy, and transforms it into gold of virginal purity, requiring only intense heat.

*Also, in his Tree of Life, page 630:* I am forced to believe there is a stone or powder that can create gold and silver; for I have several times performed a projection of one grain of it upon several thousand

### *Also*, Another rare experiment performed at the *Hague*.

There lived at the *Hague* in 1664 a silversmith named *Grill*, who was well-practiced in *alchemy* <note>original: "Alkymy"</note>, but poor, as is often the case with *chemists*. This *Grill* obtained some *Spirit of Salt* <note>original: "Spirit of Salt," a historical name for hydrochloric acid</note>—which was not prepared in the common way—from one *Caspar Knotner*, a *cloth dyer*, to use (as he said) for metals. He afterward poured this upon one pound of common lead in an open glass dish or platter, of the kind usually used for sweets or preserves <note>original: "confections or conditures"</note>. 

After two weeks, there appeared a most curious *Star* of *Silver* floating upon it, appearing as if it had been drawn with a *pencil* <note>original: "Pensel"</note> and a pair of *compasses* by some skillful artist. Because of this, the said *Grill* told us with joy that he had seen the *Signed Star* <note>original: "Signat Star," an alchemical "signature" or omen of success</note> of the *philosophers*, which he had happened to read about in the works of *Basilius* <note>Basilius Valentinus, a legendary 15th-century alchemist</note>. I, along with many others, saw this to our great wonder. 

In the meantime, the lead remaining at the bottom turned an ashy color. After seven or nine days in *July*, the *Spirit of Salt* having evaporated in the heat of the air, the *Star* settled on the lead or *residue* <note>original: "Feces"</note> at the bottom and spread itself over it, which many people saw. At last, the said *Grill* took a part of it, and from that pound of lead, he found by calculation twelve ounces of refined <note>original: "cupelled"</note> *silver*; and out of those twelve ounces, he found two ounces of the best *gold*. 

I, *Helvetius*, can show some part of that spongy lead with part of the *Star* upon it, and also some of the said *silver* and *gold*. Now, while this envious, foolish <note>original: "Silly," which in the 17th century could mean simple or ignorant</note> *Grill*—concealing his methods—endeavored to get more of that spirit of salt from *Knotner*, the said *Knotner* forgot what kind it was or else could not find it quickly. Shortly after, *Knotner* drowned, and *Grill* and his family died of the *plague*, so that no one could make further...

<summary>Johann Helvetius recounts a 1664 experiment in the Hague where a silversmith named Grill allegedly transmuted lead into silver and gold using a special "Spirit of Salt." The experiment resulted in a "Philosopher's Star" appearing in the solution, though the secret was lost when both the supplier and the smith died shortly after.</summary>
<keywords>Helvetius, The Hague, Grill, alchemy, Spirit of Salt, lead to gold, transmutation, Basilius Valentinus, silver star, plague</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Johann Helvetius’s account of alchemical experiments at the Hague, transitioning from a story about a silversmith named Grill to the beginning of his famous encounter with a mysterious stranger.</meta>

...further benefit or trial of the said progress afterward. Indeed, it would move one to admiration that the lead’s inward nature should appear in such a noble outward form by the simple maturation of the said spirit of salt <note>original: "spirit of Salt," a historical name for hydrochloric acid</note>. Neither is it less wonderful that the philosopher's stone <note>original: "Philosophers Stone," a legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and silver</note> should so suddenly transmute all metals to gold or silver, having its virtue <note>meaning its inherent power or quality</note> potentially implanted within itself and raised into an active power—just as is manifest in iron touched with a lodestone <note>original: "Load Stone," a naturally magnetized piece of the mineral magnetite</note>. 

But enough of this.

---

## CHAPTER III.

*The sooner a promise is performed, the more welcome it is. Therefore, I return to my intended history.*

On the twenty-seventh of December, 1666, in the afternoon, a stranger came to my house at the Hague. He was dressed in common clothing <note>original: "Plebeick habit," meaning the clothes of a common person</note> and possessed an honest gravity and serious authority. He was of average height <note>original: "mean Stature"</note>, with a slightly long face marked by a few small pockmarks, very black hair that was not at all curled, and a beardless chin. He was about forty-three or forty-four years of age (as I guessed) and was born in North Holland. 

After we exchanged greetings, he respectfully begged me to pardon his unannounced arrival <note>original: "rude accesses"</note>, saying he was a great lover of the chemical art <note>original: "Pyrotechnyan Art," referring to alchemy or chemistry practiced with fire</note>. He added that he had formerly endeavored to...

<summary>Helvetius reflects on the wondrous nature of alchemy and the philosopher's stone before beginning his primary narrative: a visit from a mysterious, plain-looking stranger from North Holland interested in "the Pyrotechnian Art."</summary>
<keywords>alchemy, spirit of salt, philosopher's stone, lodestone, The Hague, North Holland, Pyrotechnian Art, Johann Friedrich Helvetius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the account of Johann Helvetius's meeting with the mysterious stranger, who identifies himself as a brass founder while displaying profound alchemical knowledge.</meta>

...visited me with a friend of his, and told me he had read some of my small treatises—particularly the one against the Powder of Sympathy <note>original: "Sympathetick Powder," a remedy promoted by Sir Kenelm Digby, who claimed it could heal a wound if applied to the weapon that caused it</note> of *Sir Kenelm Digby*. He observed my doubt regarding the "philosophical mystery" <note>meaning the secrets of alchemy</note>, which prompted him to take this opportunity to ask if I could believe that a medicine existed in nature that could cure all diseases, unless the vital organs (such as the lungs, liver, etc.) were destroyed, or the predestined time of death had arrived. 

To this I replied that I had never met an Adept <term>Adept: a person who has mastered the secret of transmuting base metals into gold and creating the universal medicine</term>, nor seen such a medicine, though I had read much about it and had wished for it. Then I asked if he were a physician, but he, anticipating my question, said he was a brass founder. However, he said that from his youth he had learned many rare things in chemistry from a friend—particularly the method of extracting many medicinal arcana <note>original: "Arcana's," meaning deep secrets or powerful alchemical remedies</note> from metals by the force of fire—and he remained a lover of the art. 

After a lengthy discussion of experiments with metals, this **Elias** <note>In alchemical tradition, "Elias the Artist" was a legendary figure prophesied by Paracelsus who would appear to reveal the final secrets of chemistry</note> asked me if I would recognize the Philosopher's Stone if I saw it. I answered, "not at all," though I had read much about it in the works of **Paracelsus**, **Van Helmont**, **Basil Valentine**, and others; yet I dared not say I could recognize the "philosopher's matter" <note>the raw substance or "materia prima" used to create the Stone</note>. 

In the meantime, he took an elegant ivory box out of his breast pouch or pocket. From it, he took three heavy pieces or small lumps of the Stone, each about the size of a small walnut. They were transparent and of a pale sulfur <note>original: "Brimstone"</note> color. Sticking to them were the internal scales from the crucible <term>Crucible: a heat-resistant container used for melting metals</term> in which it appeared this most noble substance had been melted. The value of them might be judged worth about twenty tons of gold. When I had eagerly viewed and handled them for almost a quarter of an hour, and drawn from the owner many rare secrets of its admirable effects on human...

I further asked to know why the color was yellow and not red, ruby-colored, or purple, as the philosophers <note>original: "Philosophers," referring to alchemical authors</note> describe it. He answered that it did not matter, for the substance was mature and ripe enough. 

Then I humbly requested him to give me a small piece of the medicine as a lasting memory of him, even if it were only the size of a *coriander* or *hemp seed*. He immediately answered, "Oh no, no, this is not permitted, even if you would give me as many gold ducats <note>original: "Duckets," a common gold coin</note> as would fill this room. This is not because of the material value, but because of certain consequences. In fact," he said, "if it were possible for fire to be burned by fire, I would rather cast all this substance into the fiercest flames this very moment." 

But after he asked if I had another private room that looked out onto the public street, I immediately led him into the best-furnished room at the back of the house. He entered without wiping his shoes (which were covered in snow and dirt, as is the custom in *Holland*). I had no doubt then that he would give me a portion of it, or some other great secret treasure, but it was in vain. For he instead asked for a small piece of gold. 

Removing his cloak (or traveler's robe <note>original: "Pastoral habit"</note>), he opened his doublet <term>Doublet: a man’s snug-fitting buttoned jacket, common from the 14th to 17th centuries</term>, under which he wore five pieces of gold hanging from green silk ribbons. These were as large as the inner circle of a small pewter plate <term>Trencher: a flat piece of wood or metal used as a plate</term>. This gold so far surpassed my own that there was no comparison in terms of flexibility and color. The following figures with their engraved inscriptions are representations of them, which he allowed me to copy.

Deeply impressed by this, I wanted to know where and how he had obtained them. He answered, "A foreign <note>original: "outlandish"</note> friend who lived in my house for several days (claiming to be a devotee of this art who came to reveal its secrets to me) taught me various arts. First, how to make rubies, chrysolites, and sapphires, and so on, out of ordinary stones and crystals, making them much more beautiful than natural ones. He also taught me how to make *Iron Oxide* <note>original: *Crocus Martis*; an alchemical preparation of iron used in medicine and as a pigment</note> in just fifteen minutes, of which a single dose would certainly cure the plague-like dysentery <note>original: "Pestilential Dissentary (or Bloody Flux)"; a severe, infectious form of diarrhea</note>. He showed me how to make a metallic liquid that would surely cure all kinds of edema <note>original: "Dropsies"; swelling caused by excess fluid in the body</note> in four days. 

He also showed me a clear, transparent water sweeter than honey. By placing it in hot sand for two hours, it would automatically extract the essence <term>tincture</term> from garnets <note>original: "Granats"</note>, corals, glass, and similar materials—though I, Helvetius, did not actually witness this myself." My mind was drawn further, wanting to understand how such a noble essence could be extracted from metals in order to transmute them; however, like the dog in the fable who lost his piece of meat by snapping at its reflection <note>An allusion to Aesop's fable of "The Dog and the Shadow"</note> in the water, I was distracted by secondary things. 

Furthermore, he told me that his master had him bring a glass full of rainwater and some refined silver beaten into thin plates. Within fifteen minutes, the silver dissolved in the water like ice melting in heat. Then he drank half of it as a toast to me, and I drank the other half in return. It tasted no different than sweet milk, and afterwards, I felt very cheerful. I then asked if this was a "Philosophical Drink" and why we had drunk this potion. He replied that I should not be so inquisitive. Afterward, he told me that under his master's instructions, he took a piece of a lead pipe—from a gutter or a cistern—and once it was melted, he added a small amount of a sulfurous powder from his pocket, and once

...[he] again put a little more on the tip of a knife, and after a great blast of the bellows, he soon poured it onto the red stones of the kitchen chimney. It turned out to be most excellent, pure gold. He said this threw him into such a trembling amazement that he could hardly speak.

But his master then encouraged him again, saying, "Cut off a sixteenth part of this for yourself as a keepsake <note>original: "memorial"</note>, and give the rest away to the poor," which he did. He distributed such a large amount of charity <note>original: "Alms"</note> that he claimed (if my memory serves me) it went to the Church of Sparrenda. But whether he gave it all at once or at several times, or in the form of the gold mass <note>original: "Golden Masse"; a lump of unrefined gold</note> itself or in silver coin, I did not ask. 

Finally, he said (continuing the story of his master), "He taught me this almost divine art thoroughly." As soon as this history was finished, I most humbly begged that he would show me the effect of transmutation to confirm my faith in it. But he dismissed me for that time so discreetly that I was effectively denied. However, he promised to return in three weeks to show me some curious arts involving fire and the method of projection <term>projection</term>—provided it was then lawful and not forbidden.

At the end of the three weeks, he came and invited me out for an hour or two. During our walk, we discussed various secrets of nature regarding fire, but he was very cautious <note>original: "sparing"</note> when speaking of the Great Elixir <term>Great Elixir</term>. He gravely asserted that its purpose was only to magnify the sweet fame and name of the most glorious God, and that few men endeavored to sacrifice to Him through good works. He spoke this like a pastor or minister of a church. 

Every now and then, I tried to keep his attention <note>original: "kept his ears open"</note> by pleading for him to show me the metallic transmutation. I also expressed my desire that he would consider me worthy enough for him to eat, drink, and stay at my house. I pursued this so eagerly that hardly any suitor could

plead more to win his mistress from his rival <note>original: "Corrival"</note>; but he was of such a fixed and steadfast spirit that all my endeavors were frustrated. Nonetheless, I could not help telling him further that I had a suitable laboratory and materials ready and fit for an experiment, and that a promised favor was a kind of debt. "Yes, true," he said, "but I promised to teach you upon my return with this proviso: only if it were not forbidden."

At last, out of his philosophical compassion <note>original: "Philosophical commiseration"</note>, he gave me a crumb as big as a rape or turnip seed, saying, "Receive this small portion of the greatest treasure in the world, which truly few kings or princes have ever known or seen." 

But I said, "This perhaps will not transmute even four grains of lead," whereupon he told me to give it back to him. I did so, hoping for a larger piece; but cutting it in half with his fingernail, he threw one half into the fire and gave me the rest wrapped neatly in blue paper, saying, "It is still sufficient for you." 

I answered him (indeed, with a most dejected expression), "Sir, what does this mean? The other piece was already too small, and now you give me even less." 

He told me, "If you cannot manage this—considering its great potency for such a small quantity of lead—then put two drams <term>dram</term>, or half an ounce, or a little more of lead into the crucible. For no more lead should be put into the crucible than the medicine <term>medicine</term> can act upon and transmute." 

So I gave him great thanks for my diminished treasure, truly concentrated to the highest degree, and put it away carefully in my little box, saying I intended to try it the next day and would not reveal it to anyone. 

"Not so, not so," said he, "for we ought to reveal all things to the Children of Art <term>Children of Art</term>;"

...which may tend to the unique honor of God, so that they may live in the divine truth <note>original: "Theosophical truth"; knowledge of God obtained through mystical insight or alchemy.</note>, and not die in deceptive reasoning <note>original: "Sophistically"; referring to "Sophistry," or clever but false arguments.</note>. After I confessed to him that while this mass of his medicine <note>In alchemy, "the medicine" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the substance used to heal "sick" (imperfect) metals into gold.</note> was in my hands, I endeavored to scrape a little of it away with my fingernail and could not resist doing so. However, I scratched off nothing—or so very little that it was like an invisible atom—which, after cleaning it from my nail and wrapping it in paper, I threw onto lead. But I found no transmutation; instead, almost the whole mass of lead evaporated, and what remained turned into a mere glassy earth <note>A slag-like or vitrified byproduct of the heating process.</note>. 

At this unexpected outcome, he smiled and said, "You are more skilled at committing theft than at applying your medicine. If you had only wrapped your stolen prize in yellow wax to protect it from the rising fumes of the lead, it would have penetrated to the bottom of the lead and transmuted it into gold. But because you threw it directly into the fumes, the force of the vapors and their natural attraction <note>original: "Sympathetick alliance"; a belief that certain substances are naturally drawn to one another.</note> carried your medicine completely away. For gold, silver, mercury <note>original: "Quick-silver"</note>, and similar metals are corrupted and turned brittle like glass by the vapors of lead." 

Upon hearing this, I brought him the crucible <note>A ceramic or metal container used for melting materials at very high temperatures.</note> where the experiment was done, and he immediately noticed a most beautiful, saffron-colored stain <note>original: "Tincture"; in alchemy, a substance capable of infusing its color and properties into another.</note> sticking to the sides. He promised to return the next morning at nine o'clock to show me my error and prove that the medicine would indeed change lead into gold. 

Nevertheless, in the meantime, I earnestly begged him to tell me—for my own instruction—if this "philosophical work" <note>The "Great Work" (Magnum Opus) of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> was expensive or took a long time. "My friend, my friend," he said, "you are too eager to know everything in an instant. Yet I will reveal this much: neither the great expense nor the length of time required should discourage anyone,"

any; for as for the substance from which our Great Work <note>original: "Magistery"; the mastery or secret procedure of alchemy to create the Philosopher's Stone.</note> is made, I would have you know there are only two

metals and minerals from which it is prepared. But because the "Sulphur of the Philosophers" <note>In alchemy, "Sulphur" represents the active, combustible, or "soul" principle of a substance, not necessarily common sulfur.</note> is much more plentiful and abundant in minerals, it is therefore made from minerals. Then I asked again, what was the solvent <note>original: "Menstruum"; a liquid substance used to dissolve solid material.</note>, and whether the operation or work was done in glass vessels or crucibles? He answered that the solvent was a "Heavenly Salt"—or one of heavenly power—by whose benefit alone wise men dissolve the earthly metallic body. Through such a solution, the most noble *Elixir* <note>A substance believed to be capable of changing base metals into gold or prolonging life.</note> of the Philosophers is easily and instantly brought forth. However, the entire operation is performed in a crucible from beginning to end over an open fire. The whole work takes no longer than four days from start to finish, and the entire process costs no more than three florins <note>original: "Florens"; a gold coin or unit of currency.</note>. Furthermore, neither the mineral from which it was made, nor the salt by which it was performed, was of any great price. 

When I replied that the Philosophers claim in their writings that at least seven or nine months are required for this work, he answered, "Their writings are only to be understood by the true Adepts <note>original: "Adeptists"; those who have attained the final secret of alchemy.</note>; therefore, they would write nothing certain regarding the time. Indeed, without the guidance of a true Adept philosopher, no student can find the way to prepare this Great Work. For this reason, I warn and charge you (as a friend) not to throw away your money and goods hunting for this art, for you shall never find it." To which I replied, "Your master (though unknown) showed it to you; so you might perhaps reveal something to me so that, having mastered the basics, I might find the rest with little difficulty, according to the old saying: *'It is easy to add to things already invented.'*" <note>original: "It is easier to adde to a foundation"; likely referring to the Latin proverb *Facile est inventis addere*.</note>

<summary>The mysterious stranger explains that the Philosopher's Stone is made from specific minerals using a "Heavenly Salt" in a process that takes only four days. He warns Helvetius that alchemical texts are intentionally misleading and that the secret can only be learned from a true Adept.</summary>

then begin anew. He answered, "In this *Art* <note>original: "Art"; referring here to the "Great Work" of alchemy, the pursuit of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, it is quite otherwise. Unless you know the thing from head to heel—from the eggs to the apples <note>original: "ab ovo ad mala"; a Latin idiom meaning from the very beginning to the very end of a meal, and thus from start to finish.</note> (that is, from the very beginning to the very end), you know nothing. And although I have told you enough, yet you do not know how the *Philosophers* make and break open the 'Glassy Seal of *Hermes*' <note>original: "Sigillum Hermetis"; a method of sealing a glass vessel airtight by melting the neck, essential for alchemical heating.</note>. In that seal, the Sun sends forth a great splendor with its marvelous, colored metallic rays; and in that looking glass, the eyes of *Narcissus* behold the transmutable metals. For out of those rays, the true adept Philosophers gather their fire, by whose help the volatile <note>original: "Volatil"; substances that easily vaporize or change state when heated.</note> metals may be fixed into the most permanent metals, either *Gold* or *Silver*. 

But enough for now; for I intend (God willing) to meet once more tomorrow at the ninth hour (as I said) to discourse further on this philosophical subject, and I shall show you the manner of *Projection* <note>original: "Projection"; the final stage of the alchemical process where the Stone is cast onto base metal to transform it into gold.</note>." 

And having taken his leave, he left me sorrowfully waiting for him. But the next day he did not come, nor has he since. He only sent an excuse at half-past nine that morning, due to his great business, and promised to come at three in the afternoon—but he never came, nor have I heard of him since. Because of this, I began to doubt the whole matter. 

Nevertheless, late that night my wife (who was a most curious student and inquirer after the *Art* that worthy man had discussed) came soliciting and urging me to experiment with that little spark of his bounty <note>The small piece of the Philosopher's Stone given to Helvetius by the stranger.</note> in that *Art*, so that we might be more certain of the truth. She said to me, "Unless this is done, I shall have no rest nor sleep all night." But I urged her to have patience until the next morning to wait for this *Elias* <note>A reference to "Elias Artista," a legendary figure in alchemy predicted to arrive and reveal the final secrets of the craft.</note>, saying, "Perhaps he will return again to show us the right way." be made. I thought to myself, "Alas, now this man (though so divine in his speech) is found guilty of falsehood." Secondly, I blamed him for the failure of my previous attempt—when I had secretly scraped a bit of his powder under my fingernail—because it had not transformed the lead at that time. And lastly, I doubted him because he gave me such a small amount of his "Medicine" <note>original: "Medicine"; a term for the Philosopher's Stone or the substance used for transmutation.</note> (as I thought) to work upon such a large quantity of lead as he had claimed and directed. I said further to myself, "I fear, I truly fear this man has deceived me."

Nevertheless, my wife wrapped the substance in wax, and I cut half an ounce (or six drams <note>original: "Drams"; a unit of weight, roughly 1/8 of an ounce.</note>) of old lead and put it into a crucible in the fire. Once it was melted, my wife added the Medicine, which she had formed into a small pill or button. It immediately began to hiss and bubble in a perfect reaction. Within a quarter of an hour, the entire mass of lead was totally transmuted <note>original: "transmuted"; transformed from one element into another.</note> into the best and finest gold. We were all as stunned as if we had been struck by the stars <note>original: "Planets struck"; an archaic expression similar to "starstruck" or "thunderstruck," implying a sudden, overwhelming influence from the heavens or the alignment of the planets.</note>. 

Indeed, had I lived in the age of *Ovid* <note>The Roman poet Publius Ovidius Naso, famous for his work *Metamorphoses*, which details mythological transformations.</note>, there could not have been a rarer metamorphosis than this, achieved through the art of *Alchemy*. Truly, even if I had possessed the eyes of *Argus* <note>In Greek mythology, Argus Panoptes was a giant with a hundred eyes.</note>, and a hundred more besides, I could not have gazed enough upon this so admirable and almost miraculous work of nature. For after the "projection" <note>original: "projection"; the final stage of the alchemical process where the Stone is cast onto base metal.</note> was made, the melted lead displayed the rarest and most beautiful colors imaginable while in the fire. It even turned the most vibrant green; but as soon as I poured it out into an ingot <note>original: "Ingot"; a mold for casting metal into a bar or block.</note>, it took on the lively, fresh color of blood. Once cooled, it shone like the purest, most refined, and radiant gold. 

Truly, I and everyone standing with me were exceedingly startled. We ran with this "aurified" <note>original: "Aurified"; turned into gold.</note> lead—while it was still hot—to the goldsmith. He marveled at its purity, and after a short trial of touch <note>original: "trial of Touch"; a method of testing the purity of gold by rubbing it on a touchstone and comparing the streak to samples of known quality.</note>, he judged it to be the most excellent gold in the whole

<meta>This page continues Johann Friedrich Helvetius’s account of his successful alchemical transmutation. <page-num>24</page-num>

world, and offered most willingly to give fifty florins for every ounce of it.

The next day, a rumor went about *The Hague* and spread abroad, so that many illustrious persons and students gave me friendly visits for the sake of the gold. Among the rest was the general assay-master <note>original: "Say-master"; an official responsible for testing the purity and weight of coins and precious metals.</note> or examiner of the coins of this province of *Holland*, Mr. *Porelius*. He, along with others, earnestly besought me to put some part of it through all their customary trials, which I did—largely to gratify my own curiosity.

Thereupon we went to Mr. *Brechtel*, a silversmith, who first tested it by quartation <note>original: "per Quartam"; a refining process where gold is alloyed with three parts silver so that nitric acid can effectively reach and dissolve the silver, leaving pure gold behind.</note>. That is, he mixed three or four parts of silver with one part of the said gold, then rolled it into thin plates, filed it, or granulated it. He then added a sufficient quantity of *aqua fortis* <note>original: "Aqua Fort"; nitric acid.</note> to it, which immediately dissolved the silver and allowed the gold to settle to the bottom as a powder. After the liquid was poured off, and the powder of gold was dulcified <note>original: "dulcified"; washed with water to neutralize and remove any trace of the acid.</note> with water and then melted back into a solid mass, it became excellent gold. **And whereas we feared a loss of material, we found that each dram of the original gold had actually increased; it had transformed a scruple <note>original: "Scruple"; a small unit of weight, roughly 1.3 grams.</note> of the silver into gold because of its great and excellent, abounding tincture <note>original: "Tincture"; in alchemy, the spiritualized energy or "medicine" within the Philosopher’s Stone that possesses the power to transform other metals.</note>.**

But then, doubting further whether the silver was sufficiently separated from the gold, we instantly mixed it with seven parts of antimony. We melted this and poured it into a conical mold, then blew off the regulus <note>original: "Regulus"; the pure metallic heart of a substance that settles at the bottom of a crucible after smelting.</note> on a cupel <note>original: "Test"; a shallow, porous dish used in assaying to separate precious metals from base metals.</note>. At first, we thought we had lost eight grains of our gold. However, after we blew away the rest of the antimony or excess slag <note>original: "Scoria"; the dross or waste material left over after a metal is smelted.</note>, we found nine grains of gold to replace our eight missing grains. Although this gold was somewhat pale and silver-like, it easily recovered its full color afterward. So, in the most rigorous test of fire, we lost nothing at all of this gold, but gained as I have already stated;

<meta>catchword: aforesaid ;</meta>

<summary>Helvetius describes the public verification of his gold by the assay-master of Holland and a silversmith. Through several rigorous chemical tests, including quartation and smelting with antimony, they discovered that the gold was not only pure but had actually increased in weight by transforming some of the test silver into gold.</summary>

May He bring him to eternal bliss and happiness in His heavenly kingdom after the end of his full pilgrimage in this life, for the help and relief of Christendom and the whole world. Amen.

## CHAPTER 4.

->*I now turn to the dialogue between* Elias *the Artist and the Physician to explain what has happened and all other matters.*<-

Elias. God save you, *Helvetius*. I have heard of your diligent <note>original: "curious"; in this period, "curious" meant careful, inquisitive, or scholarly.</note> search for the secrets of nature and have read your books—particularly the one against Sir *Kenelm Digby's Sympathetic Powder*, in which he boasts of healing all wounds from a distance. <note>Sir Kenelm Digby (1603–1665) was a courtier and philosopher who famously promoted a "powder of sympathy" believed to heal wounds by applying it to the blood-stained clothing or the weapon rather than the patient.</note> Truly, I take incredible delight in all such things which we see naturally implanted in creatures as if in a mirror, whether they are sympathetic or antipathetic <note>original: "Sympathetick or Antipathetrick"; terms referring to natural attractions or repulsions between substances, a central concept in early modern science.</note>. For the inexhaustible treasures of the Divine Light and Godhood (which have been abundantly granted to us) can be perfectly understood through the creatures living under the sky, in the womb of the earth, or brought forth from the seas. All their gifts and powers (inherent within them) were intended to be beneficial for restoring health and providing aid to mortal man.

*Physician.* Sir, you are the most welcome guest; for a philosophical discussion of nature is the only refreshment and healthy <note>original: "Salutiferous"; health-giving or beneficial.</note> nourishment for my spirit. Please, come into this room.

*Elias.* Sir, it seems you have a whole laboratory of the fiery art of *Vulcan* <note>Vulcan was the Roman god of fire and blacksmithing; in alchemy, it refers to the use of the furnace and heat.</note> here, and perhaps all the spagyric medicines <note>original: "Spagyrical Medicines"; alchemical medicines produced by the "spagyric" method of separating a substance into its elements and then purifying and recombining them.</note> most precisely extracted from the mineral kingdom.

<summary>Helvetius introduces a dialogue with the mysterious visitor, Elias the Artist, who praises Helvetius’s scientific curiosity and discusses the divine power hidden within the natural world.</summary>
<keywords>Elias the Artist, Helvetius, Kenelm Digby, Sympathetic Powder, alchemy, Vulcan, spagyric medicine, natural philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Physician (Helvetius) and the mysterious stranger, Elias the Artist, focusing on the simplicity of true medicine versus the complexity of contemporary practice.</meta>

( 27 )

kingdom. But Sir, for what purpose are there so many medicines? We could restore human health much sooner and more safely using only a few, provided the illness is not fatal—whether due to a birth defect, the decay of a vital organ, or the total depletion of the vital moisture <note>original: "radical humidity"; in humoral medicine, this was the essential fluid believed to sustain life and heat in the body.</note>. In such hopeless cases, neither traditional Galenic cures <note>original: "Galenical Cures"; treatments based on the ancient Greek physician Galen, which usually relied on balancing the four humors using herbs.</note> nor Paracelsian tinctures <note>original: "Paracelsical Tinctures"; chemical medicines derived from minerals and metals, following the theories of Paracelsus.</note> can be helpful. But it is not so in ordinary diseases, where men are nevertheless often forced to depart from this sweet light to join the dead before their destined time, simply for want of speedy and powerful remedies.

*Physician.* Sir, I gather from your discourse that you are either a physician or an expert student of chemistry. Truly, I believe there are more excellent medicines—and even a universal medicine—which might prolong life until its appointed end and heal all disturbances in the human body. But who can show the way to such a fountain from which such a medicinal juice may be obtained? Perhaps no one among men.

*Elias.* Truly, I am only a brass-founder. Yet, almost from my cradle, my inner spirit <note>original: "Genius"; a person's natural inclination or a guiding spirit.</note> prompted me to search for wonders in the fiery art <note>A reference to alchemy and metallurgy, which both rely on the use of fire and furnaces.</note>, and I have diligently searched through the internal nature of metals. Although I now refrain from constant labor and precise investigation, such work and those who love it are still delightful to me. I believe that the most high, great, and good God will, in this age of ours, grant his alchemical <note>original: "Spagyrick"; refers to the alchemical process of separating and recombining substances to create medicines.</note> children the mysteries of metals for free—if they pray and labor to attain them.

*Physician.* I grant that God grants his wonderful gifts for free, yet he has seldom given, nor does he easily sell, this medicinal nectar to his children for nothing.

<summary>Elias argues that true medicine should be simple and powerful, preventing premature death, while identifying himself as a humble brass-founder with a lifelong passion for alchemical secrets. The Physician expresses doubt that the "universal medicine" can be easily found, despite Elias's belief that God is revealing these secrets to the faithful.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the conversation between Elias the Artist and the physician (Helvetius). Elias has just mentioned that he is a brass-founder by trade but a student of alchemy by calling. ( 28 )

For we know for certain that countless alchemists <note>original: "Chymists"</note> have done, and continue to do, a useless task—like drawing water through a sieve—while they presume to prepare the universal Stone of Philosophers. From the books of those successful Masters <note>original: "triumphing Adeptists"; those believed to have successfully created the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, no one can learn the manner of preparing it or identify their "first matter" <note>The "Prima Materia," the fundamental, formless starting material required for the alchemical Great Work.</note>. And while one person searches at the lowest root and foot of the mountain, he never ascends to the highest peak, where alone he can eat and drink the food and drink of the gods <note>original: "Ambrofia and Nectar"</note> of the Great Sages <note>original: "Macro-Sophists"</note> or Philosophers. In the meantime, if a good physician lacks that universal Elixir, it is his duty to keep a pure and safe conscience and apply such restoring remedies to diseases in which he certainly finds the effect and power <note>original: "Virtue"</note> of curing them. Therefore, in all desperate diseases, I use such simple medicines <note>original: "Medicaments"</note> that the patients either recover quickly or are put on the path toward their former better health.

For various kinds of salts are produced in the glands <note>original: "Glandules"</note> and lymphatic vessels after the decay of this or that food. These later bloom into various humors <note>In traditional medicine, the four "humors" (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) were thought to determine a person's health and temperament.</note> and cause either internal or external illnesses. Experience teaches us that as there are many different constitutions or temperaments <note>original: "Complexions"</note>, so there are many variations of diseases, even if it is the very same disease in general. We see this in those who drink wine, where different effects immediately show themselves.

For instance, after drinking wine, *Peter* immediately begins to be angry and furious; on the contrary, *Paul* seems to have the timidness of a lamb; but *Matthew* sings, and *Luke* weeps.

*Also.* From the contagious poison of scurvy <note>original: "Scorbutical poison"</note>, the vital fluid <note>original: "Radical Juice"; the essential moisture believed to sustain life.</note> of *Peter* in his lymphatic vessels and glands is turned into acidity, which obstructs

the passages and organs of the entire body. From this, discolored azure or sky-blue spots appear under the skin; however, during a plague, they break out looking like grains of pepper.

But the fluid <note>original: "Juice"</note> of these same parts in a person like *Paul* is changed into a piercing bitterness. This causes red spots like fleabites to grow on the skin under the arms and legs; but during the plague, these become carbuncles <note>Painful, pus-filled boils caused by bacterial infection.</note>.

<meta>This page precedes the previously modernized text. For we know certainly that infinite numbers of chemists <note>original: "Chymists"; in this context, Helvetius refers to alchemists searching for the Philosopher's Stone.</note> have and do still "draw water through a sieve" <note>An idiom meaning to perform a useless or impossible task.</note>, while they presume to prepare the **Philosopher's Stone** <note>original: "universal Stone of Philosophers"; a legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and curing all diseases.</note>. In the meantime, it is the duty of a good physician—for lack of that universal *Elixir*—to keep a pure and safe conscience and apply to diseases such restoring remedies in which he certainly finds the effect and power <note>original: "Virtue"; the inherent medicinal efficacy of a substance.</note> of curing them. Therefore, in all desperate diseases, I use such very simple medications <note>original: "Medicaments"</note> that the patients either speedily recover or are brought into some way toward their former better health.

For there are various kinds of salts generated in the glands <note>original: "Glandules"</note> and lymphatic vessels after the rotting <note>original: "putrefaction"</note> of this or that food, which afterwards flourish out into various humors <note>The bodily fluids—blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile—which early modern medicine believed determined health and personality.</note> and cause either internal or external illnesses. Experience teaches us that just as there are many different constitutions or temperaments <note>original: "Complexions"</note>, there are also many variations of diseases, although it may be the very same disease in general. We see this in those who drink wine, where different effects immediately show themselves.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of how different "scorbutic" (scurvy-related) poisons in the body require specific herbal correctors. Having established that different patients (Peter, Paul, Matthew, and Luke) exhibit different symptoms, Helvetius now identifies the specific plant-based remedies for each, leading into a debate with Elias about whether a "universal medicine" exists in the mineral kingdom.</meta>

( 30 )

Antipathy <note>A natural opposition or incompatibility between substances.</note> is also observed between the herb fumitory and brooklime <note>original: "Baccabungy"; the Latin name is *Veronica beccabunga*.</note>. Therefore:

The remedy <note>original: "Corrector".</note> for **Peter's** scurvy-related, staining, salty, and sour poison is made with the bitter volatile salt of the herb scurvy-grass.

The remedy for **Paul's** scurvy-related, staining, salty, and bitter poison is made with the fixed sour salt of the herb sorrel.

And the remedy for **Matthew's** scurvy-related, salty, staining, sweet, and moistening poison is made with the help of the fixed bitter and drying sulfur <note>In early chemistry, "sulfur" was one of the three primary principles, representing the oily or combustible part of a substance.</note> of the herb fumitory.

But the remedy for **Luke's** scurvy-related, staining, salty, sharp, and drying poison is made by the help of the sweet, moistening mercury <note>In this context, "mercury" refers to the spirituous or fluid principle of a plant, not the metal.</note> of the herb brooklime <note>original: "Baccabungy".</note> or red cabbage <note>original: "Red Coleworts".</note>. Just as it is very easy to judge the specific internal remedy from the external "signature" <note>The "Doctrine of Signatures" was the belief that a plant's physical appearance gave a clue to its medicinal use.</note> of those herbs, so it is against these various scurvy-related diseases. Truly, my friend; if this is well observed, a wise physician will doubt the existence of a universal medicine.

**Elias.** I shall easily grant all that you have argued, yet very few physicians follow this method. In the meantime, it is not at all impossible that there is also a universal medicine in the kingdom of minerals (being the highest kingdom), by whose benefit alone we may achieve everything you have described using many remedies from the lower kingdom of plants <note>original: "Vegetables".</note>. But our most great and good God, for some weighty reasons, has not given this kind of magnificent, divine gift <note>original: "Charismal gift".</note> or superior science to all *philosophers* indiscriminately; rather, He has revealed it to a few, though all the adepts <note>original: "Adeptists"; those who have mastered the secrets of alchemy.</note> agree that this science is true, and that no one ought to doubt its truth in the least.

<meta>catchword: Physician.</meta>

<summary>Helvetius explains how specific herbs (scurvy-grass, sorrel, fumitory, and brooklime) treat different manifestations of scurvy based on their chemical principles. Elias counters by suggesting that while herbal medicine is varied, a "universal medicine" might still exist within the mineral kingdom, known only to a few divinely inspired adepts.</summary>
<keywords>scurvy, herbalism, Doctrine of Signatures, alchemy, universal medicine, mineral kingdom, fumitory, brooklime, sorrel, scurvy-grass, medical chemistry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the debate between the Physician and Elias regarding the existence of a universal medicine. While the Physician argues that differences in age, sex, and the specific stage of a disease make a single cure impossible, Elias suggests that while some herbs act as "near-universal" cures for specific symptoms, the true "universal medicine" of the alchemists operates on a much higher level by reviving the body's vital spirits.</meta>

( 31 )

**Physician:** Sir, besides the things I have mentioned, there are other observations that strongly oppose the operation of a universal medicine. This is partly due to a person's age and strength, and partly because of their sex and other circumstances. There is a clear difference between the delicate and the strong—whether by nature or upbringing—and between males and females, or young men and young women. One must also consider the beginning, middle, or end of a disease, and it must be known if the illness is long-standing <note>original: "inveterate"</note> or has only recently attacked the patient. Finally, we must know if the ferment <note>In 17th-century medicine, "ferment" referred to the chemical processes in the body, such as digestion or the spread of disease, thought to be like the action of yeast.</note> in one disease is increasing or in another is decreasing. For this activity of the ferment occurs in the stomach or intestines. Indeed, there are many arguments against a universal medicine, and few physicians have the spectacles of **Thomas Didymus** <note>A reference to "Doubting Thomas" (Thomas a Didymus), suggesting that few doctors have the clear vision or evidence needed to believe in such a cure.</note> at their fingertips.

**Elias:** You have argued very philosophically; for "there are as many opinions as there are men." <note>A common Latin proverb: *Quot homines, tot sententiae*.</note> Just as sweet music does not please every **Midas's** <note>In Greek mythology, King Midas was judged to have poor taste when he preferred the music of Pan over Apollo.</note> ears, and the same foods and drinks do not please every palate, so too the judgments of unskilled people vary regarding this universal medicine—both for human and metallic bodies. Certainly, the way this medicine works differs greatly from specific <note>original: "particular"</note> medicines. Nevertheless, some of these are almost universal, or are esteemed as such: for example, the herb scurvy-grass cures all kinds of scurvy marked with blue <note>original: "Azure"</note> spots; sorrel cures every scurvy with red spots; brooklime <note>original: "Beccabungia".</note> is used for atrophy <note>Wasting of the body.</note> or the consumptive kind; and fumitory is used for tumors of another kind. This is especially true for those physicians who hold the observations you mentioned in high regard. Besides, there is a vast difference between the universal medicine of true philosophers, which revives all the vital spirits <note>In early medicine, the "vital spirits" were thought to be a subtle, vapor-like substance that carried life and sensation throughout the body.</note>, and the

<meta>catchword: particular</meta>

<summary>The Physician argues against a universal cure based on the biological diversity of patients and the complexity of diseases, while Elias defends the concept by distinguishing between specific herbal remedies and the alchemical universal medicine that restores the body's life force.</summary>
<keywords>universal medicine, scurvy-grass, sorrel, brooklime, fumitory, vital spirits, ferment, Thomas Didymus, Midas, medical philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between the Physician and Elias. Elias is explaining that while standard "particular" medicines only treat specific symptoms or superficial "humours," the true universal medicine works by revitalizing the body's internal life force, or "vital spirits," to combat even the most deadly diseases like the plague.</meta>

(32)

[A] particular medicine <note>original: "Medicament"</note> provides only a minor cure, where only the venom of humours <term>According to the medical theory of the time, the body was governed by four "humours" or fluids; imbalances or "boiling" of these caused disease.</term> boiling against nature—in one man sour, in another bitter, in one salty <note>original: "Saline"</note>, in another sharp—is corrected. And if these corruptions are not immediately removed by the usual emunctories <term>Organs or canals in the body that carry off waste, such as the pores, kidneys, or bowels.</term> of the mouth, nostrils, stool, urine, or sweat, then certainly the corruption of one disease begets another. For every spark of fire that has fuel and is not quenched will grow into the greatest conflagration. 

But if there is a defect in the movements of the vital spirits <term>In early medicine, these were thought to be refined substances in the blood that acted as the "spark of life" and moved the body.</term>, then it is impossible to fix this with "particulars" <note>remedies that treat specific symptoms rather than the whole body</note>. Therefore, it concerns every conscientious physician to learn how he may promote the movement of the vital spirits toward a natural, digestible heat. This is most safely and best performed by our universal medicine, by which the sick are notably revived <note>original: "recreated"</note>. 

For as soon as this more-than-perfect medicine removes the "mortifying seeds" <note>the underlying causes of disease and decay</note>, Nature is restored, and so lost health is recovered. This happens only through a harmonious sympathy between the medicine and the vital spirits. For this reason, the Adepts <term>Alchemists who have reached the highest level of knowledge and successfully created the Philosopher's Stone.</term> call it the Mystery of Nature, the defense of old age, and the cure for all sicknesses—yes, even of the very plague and pestilence. Because this medicine is a kind of *Salamander* <note>In alchemy, the salamander was a creature believed to live in fire; here, it represents a medicine that can withstand the "fire" of disease or fever.</note>, it communicates its virtue and—like a *Salamander*—allows a man to live until his last appointed time, despite all the fiery epidemic darts of the angry heavens or their malevolent influences <note>Astrological "influences" from the stars and planets were then believed to cause outbreaks of disease.</note>.

*Physician: Sir, I understand from your discourse that this medicine does nothing to correct depraved or corrupt humours, but only works by strengthening the vital spirits and our balsamic nature <note>The body's innate power to heal and preserve itself, often compared to the preserving qualities of balsam.</note>. However, other practical chemists <note>original: "Chymists"</note> teach how to separate the impure from the pure, and how to "ripen the unripe"—to make the bitter become a little sour or acidic,*

<sig>F</sig>

and the sour <note>original: "fower"</note> sweet, and so to turn sharp into mild, mild into sharp, sour into sweet, and sweet into sour. I also understand you to say that this universal medicine cannot prolong life beyond its predestined <note>original: "prefixed"</note> time, but only preserves it from all venom and deadly sickness. This agrees with the common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> belief that life depends only upon the will of God. But putting those things aside, my question is still this: can a man's original nature be converted into a new nature? For example, can a slothful man be changed into a diligent, nimble man, and a man who is naturally melancholy <term>Melancholy was one of the four "humours" or temperaments in early medicine, associated with a pensive, sad, or sluggish disposition.</term> be made a merry man, or something similar?

*Elias*: Not at all, Sir. No medicine has the power to transform the nature of man in such a way, any more than wine drunk by various <note>original: "divers"</note> men changes a person's nature. Rather, it only provokes or draws out <note>original: "deduceth"</note> what is already in a man potentially and brings it into action. For the universal medicine works by refreshing <note>original: "recreating"</note> the vital spirits <term>The internal "spark" or life-force believed to animate the body and mind.</term> and so restores the health that was suppressed for a time. In the same way, the heat of the sun never transforms <note>original: "transmutes"</note> the herbs and flowers, but instead stirs up their potential powers to become active. A man of a melancholy temperament is restored to his natural melancholy disposition, and a merry man becomes merry again. 

Thus, in all desperate diseases, it is an immediate and most excellent preservative. Indeed, if life could be prolonged, then *Hermes* <term>Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary, mythical founder of alchemy.</term>, *Paracelsus* <term>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541), a famous Swiss physician and alchemist.</term>, *Trevisan* <term>Bernard Trevisan (1406–1490), an Italian alchemist who spent his life searching for the Philosopher's Stone.</term>, and many others who possessed this medicine would never have suffered the tyranny of death, but would have prolonged their lives perhaps to this very day. It would therefore be the act of a madman to believe that any medicine in the world could prolong life longer than the limits God has set.

<meta>This page continues the conversation between the Physician and Elias. <page-num>( 34 )</page-num>

All you have said concerning the Universal Medicine is as regular as it is fundamental. Yet until I can prepare it myself, it does me no good. Indeed, some illustrious men have written about it so cautiously in obscure riddles <note>original: "dark Ænygma's"</note> that very few can understand their process to the end. Even if one could purchase the works of all these authors, this short life might be consumed in the effort without ever attaining the goal. It remains, therefore, only to pray and work—*Pray and work, for God gives at every hour* <note>original: "Ora & labora, Deus dat omni hora"</note>—Work and Pray, God gives every day.

*Elias*: Indeed, this "Art of Arts" can seldom be learned from books without a demonstration from a true adept <note>original: "Adeptist"</note>.

But setting this aside, let us turn to the transmutation of metals by that most noble tincture <term>In alchemy, a "tincture" was a potent substance—often a liquid form of the Philosopher's Stone—believed to be capable of changing the properties of base metals into gold or silver.</term> about which many have written; but it is true that few students <note>original: "Disciples"</note> attain this secret <note>original: "Arcanum"</note>.

*Physician*: Because of your convincing arguments and my own previous experiments, I believe all you say. For Dr. Kuffler, using a tincture made from one ounce of gold applied <note>original: "projected"</note> to two ounces of silver, reportedly transmuted an ounce and a half into the purest gold, a third of the remainder into "white gold," and the rest remained the purest silver imaginable. Van Helmont's experiment proves the same thing. But especially notable is the experiment of Alexander Scotus and Count Russe, well known in Prague; as you can see here in the inscriptions made before the Holy Roman Emperor Ferdinand III <note>original: "Cæsar Ferdinando the Third"</note>. In that instance, with just one grain of the tincture, three pounds of mercury were transmuted into the noblest, pure gold. Yet I confess I have never seen a true adept or a successful projection <term>Projection is the final stage of the alchemical process, where the Philosopher's Stone or tincture is cast upon a molten base metal to transmute it into a precious metal.</term> performed, and therefore I cannot absolutely conclude that these things are true.

*Elias*: My friend, the art will remain true, whe-

...whether you believe it or not. For example: consider the singular, exalted sulfurous <term>sulfurous: In alchemy, "sulfur" refers to an active, combustible, or spiritual principle or "soul" of a substance, rather than the chemical element</term> power in the loadstone <term>loadstone: a naturally magnetized piece of the mineral magnetite</term>. By its touch alone, it passes a sympathetic power into sulfurous iron to make it another magnet or loadstone. The same thing happens with the Philosopher’s Stone, which contains everything that wise men seek. Now, since their writings are so numerous and obscure, it is desirable that a short, concise summary <term>laconic short epitome: a brief and focused summary</term> were extracted from them all. This would allow the said Art to be clearly demonstrated in a short time, with little labor and expense, providing an easy transition <note>original: "Transite"</note> to the best authors. *Physician*: Is this glassy yellow mass truly it? I fear you are only joking or playing with me.

*Elias*: Yes, truly. You now hold in your hands the most precious thing in the world, the true Philosopher’s Stone. None was ever more real or could be better, nor shall anyone ever have another. I myself have worked it from the very beginning to the very end.

Then, stepping into a more private room, he showed me these five pieces of pure gold, made out of lead by the philosophical tincture <term>philosophical tincture: a substance, often a liquid or powder derived from the Philosopher's Stone, used to "stain" or transmute base metals into gold</term>. *Physician*: Sir, I cannot judge; but it seems you did not learn this by yourself, but had a master who instructed you how to make it. Now I beg you, sir, bestow a little crumb of it upon me—even if it is only as much as a coriander or hemp seed—just enough to transmute four grains of lead into gold.

*Elias*: I confess that an honest, good man first showed me the possibility, and then the art and manner to pre—

...prepare the medicine, but to give you any of this medicine is not lawful, even if I were offered as many ducats <term>ducats: high-value gold coins used throughout Europe</term> as would fill this room. To prove this, I would throw it all into the fire right now to be consumed, if it were possible for fire to destroy fire <note>In alchemy, the Philosopher's Stone was often described as being "fixed" or "incombustible," meaning it had already been purified by fire and could not be further changed by it.</note>. Until your return, I shall occupy myself with what has been revealed regarding both your medicine and yourself. But I fear, sir, that if any king, prince, or ruler should learn of your fame (which God forbid), they might perhaps imprison and torture you until you reveal the entire secret to them.

*Elias:* I never showed the Stone to anyone in the world but to you, except for one elderly man, and from now on I shall not show it to anyone else. Even if any King or other person (which I hope God will not permit) should torture me on the rack <term>Rack: a torture device that stretches the victim's limbs</term> or burn me alive, I would not reveal it to them, either directly or indirectly—unlike the many wandering <note>original: "circum-feraneous"</note> physicians, charlatans <term>Mountebanks: people who sold fraudulent medicines, often from a public stage</term>, vagabonds, and others who pretend to do so.

*Physician:* Good Sir, tell me in the meantime <note>original: "Interim"</note> who are the best authors, since by your experience you are best able to judge.

*Elias:* Indeed, Doctor, I have not read many books; but among those I have read, none are more curious than *Cosmopolite Sendivogius* <note>Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636), a famous Polish alchemist and chemist</note>, the *Dutch Burger-Wereld* <note>Likely a reference to a Dutch alchemical work like "Der Burger-Welt"</note>, and *Brother Basilius’s Twelve Keys* <note>Basil Valentine, a legendary 15th-century alchemist whose "Twelve Keys" outlined the steps to creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>. I can lend you *Sendivogius* when I return. In his obscure words the Truth lies hidden, even as our tincture <term>tincture: a concentrated alchemical substance used to transform or "stain" base metals into gold</term> lies enclosed within the minerals and metallic bodies.

*Physician:* Sir, I give you my most hearty thanks for your exceeding kindness and love. I believe that marvelous and effective essences and tinctures lie hidden in metals and minerals, under the external rinds and shells of their bodies. However, I find few so expert in the use of fire that they know how to extract their "kernel" philosophically <note>In alchemy, "philosophically" refers to the secret, spiritual, or symbolic methods of the art rather than common chemical practice</note>. For (as Isaac Holland <note>Isaac Hollandus, a celebrated 15th-century Dutch alchemist</note> writes) the outward body of every animal, vegetable, and mineral is like a terrestrial province, within which excellent spiritual essences retreat and dwell. Therefore, it is necessary that the Sons of Art <term>Sons of Art: a traditional name for practitioners of alchemy</term> should know how—by some saline, fit, and suitable ferment (one pleasant and agreeable to the metallic nature)—to tame and subdue, dissolve, separate, and concentrate not only the metallic magnetic virtue with which to transform <note>original: "Tinge"</note> metals, but also the philosoph—

<summary>Elias refuses to reveal the secret of the Philosopher's Stone even under threat of death and recommends key alchemical texts to the Physician, who reflects on the spiritual essences hidden within physical matter.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the conversation between Elias and the Physician. The previous page ended with the Physician expressing his belief that it is possible to work "marvelously" to achieve the alchemical goal, and this page begins mid-word with the conclusion of "philosophically."</meta>

( 38 )

...philosophically to multiply it <note>original: "the same"</note>, in its golden or silvery homogeneity <term>Homogeneity: here, this refers to a state where the substance is uniform in its nature and quality, being purely of the essence of gold or silver</term>. For we see that the bodies of all creatures are not only easily destroyed, but as soon as they cease to live, they hurry to their graves in putrefaction—that is to say <note>original: "viz."</note>, to their old chaos and the darkness of *Orcus* <note>original: "Orcus"; in Roman mythology, Orcus was a god of the underworld. In an alchemical context, it refers to the state of primordial matter or the void before creation</note>, where they were before they were brought to light by Creation in this world. But alas, who or what man can or will show us this art in the metallic kingdom?

*Elias:* Sir, I confess you judge correctly regarding the natural destruction of things; and if it is God's pleasure, He can (through me) send someone—sooner than you hope—to show you the manner to destroy metals and minerals in a true philosophical <term>Philosophical: in alchemy, "philosophical" methods are the secret, spiritual, or chemical processes known only to true practitioners, as opposed to common chemistry</term> manner, and to gather their inward souls. In the meantime, pray for the blessing of this great God, who does all things as He pleases. To Him I recommend you—His watchful eyes are always open over all His regenerated sons, in and through Christ Jesus. So, be assured that I am your friend. And once more, farewell.

***

Thus, my friend *Elias*, taking his leave, left me for three weeks, and has remained away until this very day. Nevertheless, as a spur to my thoughts, he impressed all these things deep in my mind; and *Paracelsus* <note>Theophrastus von Hohenheim, known as Paracelsus (1493–1541), was a Swiss physician and alchemist who argued that the purpose of alchemy was to create medicine rather than just gold</note> confirmed them, saying that in, with, of, and by metals spiritualized and cleansed, perfect metals are made—as well as the living Gold and Silver of the Philosophers, for both human and metallic bodies.

<summary>Elias departs after promising the Physician that God may soon reveal the secret of extracting the "souls" of metals, leaving the Physician to reflect on the teachings of Paracelsus regarding spiritualized metals.</summary>
<keywords>Elias, Paracelsus, metallic kingdom, putrefaction, Orcus, inward souls, living gold and silver, philosophical manner</keywords>

...days. Therefore, if this guest—my friend—had taught me how to prepare this spiritual and celestial salt he spoke of, by which I might (so to speak) gather the spiritual rays of the Sun or Moon out of physical metallic substances within their own matrix <term>Matrix: in alchemy, a womb-like environment or substance where a thing originates or is formed</term>, then truly, by his own light he would have so enlightened me that I would have known how—magnetically and by a sympathetic power—the internal souls of other imperfect physical metals might be clarified and "tinged" <term>Tinged: in alchemy, "tincture" or "tinging" refers to the process of transforming a base metal into a noble one by coloring its essence</term>. For Elias told me that the *Calybs* <term>Calybs: from the Latin "chalybs" for steel or iron; used by the alchemist Sendivogius to describe a "magnetic" substance that attracts celestial influence</term> of *Sendivogius* <note>original: "Sendivogius"; Michael Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a famous Polish alchemist and physician</note> was the true mercurial metallic humidity. With its help—and without any corrosive substance—an artist might separate the fixed rays of the Sun or Moon from their own bodies in a naked fire and an open crucible. He could then make them volatile and mercurial, suitable for a "dry philosophical tincture" (as Elias partially shared and showed me before he left) to transmute metals.

For all learned chemists must agree that *pyrotechny* <term>Pyrotechny: the art of using fire in chemical or metallurgical processes; an early term for chemistry</term> is the mother and nurse of many noble sciences and arts. They can easily judge from the colors of the metallic "chaos" <note>original: "chaos"; here referring to the confused, unrefined mixture of minerals in a furnace</note> in the fire which metallic body is present. And truly, every day, metals and transparent stones are still created in the depths of the Earth from their own noble, vaporous source, combined with a spiritual, transforming sulfurous seed within various salty environments. For common sulfur (or the sulfur of any pure or impure metal while still joined with its own body), when mingled only with saltpeter <term>Saltpeter: potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in many early chemical experiments and gunpowder</term> in the burning heat of fire, will be easily changed into...

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transmutation of metals, specifically the chemical and spiritual processes involved in transforming "fixed Earth" and the sympathetic relationships between different substances.</meta>

(40)

...the hardest and most stable <term>original: "fixed"; resistant to change or the effects of fire</term> Earth. This Earth is afterward easily changed by the air into very clear water. Later, by a stronger fire—depending on whether pure or impure metallic sulfur <note>original: "Sulphur"; in alchemy, sulfur represents the principle of soul and combustibility</note> is mixed in—this water is turned into glass, colored with various and very beautiful colors. 

In a similar way, a chicken is generated and hatched out of the white of an egg by a gentle, natural heat. Thus, from the seminal "Bond of Life" of any metal, a new and much more noble metal is created by a heat suitable to the nature of an oily <term>original: "fatty"; likely referring to the alchemical concept of sulfur as a flammable "oil" or essence</term> fire. However, few chemists <note>original: "Chymists"; at this time, the term covered both alchemists and early chemical scientists</note> know perfectly how the internal virtues of metals—which always move magnetically according to their harmony or discord—are distinguished. They do not know why one metal has such a singular sympathy or antipathy <term>the alchemical belief that substances naturally attract or repel one another</term> with another metal, as is seen remarkably in the magnet with iron, in mercury with gold, and in silver with copper. 

Similarly, a notable antipathy is found in some, such as lead against tin, iron against gold, antimony against silver, and again, lead against mercury. There are 600 such notes on sympathy and antipathy in the animal and vegetable kingdoms, as various authors have written.

Thus, honest reader, I have printed here what I have seen and done. For, along with Seneca <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – AD 65), a Roman Stoic philosopher who famously said he only wanted to learn so that he could teach</note>, I desire to know things only so that I may teach others; indeed, if wisdom were given on the condition that it must be kept secret, I would reject it. If anyone should still remain doubtful, let them believe with a living faith in Christ Crucified. Through him, let them become a "new creature" through the strict path of spiritual rebirth <term>original: "regeneration"</term>. Let them be fixed there in hope and practice true love and charity toward their neighbor until their life is justly, chastly, and holily finished. By doing so, they may safely sail through the wicked and shameless...

# THE
# GOLDEN ASS
## Well managed,

->AND<-

## *MIDAS* Restored to Reason.

Or a new Alchemical <note>original: "Chymical"</note> Light appearing as a morning star <note>original: "day Star"</note> of comfort to all experiencing oppression or calamities—including the uneducated as well as the learned, and both men and women—to ease their burdens and provide for their families.

->WHEREIN<-

The Golden Fleece <note>A mythological symbol used by alchemists to represent the ultimate secret of making gold.</note> is demonstrated to the blind world, showing that good gold may be found in cold regions as well as hot (though better in hot), both inside and outside the entire globe of the earth, and can be profitably extracted. Thus, in all places where there is any sand, stones, gravel, or flints, you cannot even step without finding both gold and the true material for the Philosophers' Stone <note>A legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</note>. And this is a "work for women and a game for children" <note>original: "Work of Women and play of Children." This is a common alchemical motto (Latin: *opus mulierum et ludus puerorum*) suggesting that once the secret method is known, the work is easy and requires little physical strength.</note>.

*Written at Amsterdam, 1669, by* John Rudolph Glauber, *the bright sun of our age and lover of mankind; like a true* Elijah <note>original: "Elias." This refers to "Elias Artista," a predicted messianic figure in alchemy who would reveal all secrets and usher in a golden age.</note> *riding on this Golden Ass in a fiery chariot.*

And translated from Latin into English, in brief notes, 1670, by *W. C., Esquire*—a true lover of art and nature, and a well-wisher to all people, especially to the poor, distressed "Household of Faith" <note>A biblical term for the community of believers.</note>; the true Catholic <note>In this context, "Catholic" refers to its literal meaning of "universal" rather than the specific Roman Catholic denomination.</note> Church and the body of Christ, dispersed through many forms of religion throughout the whole world, like the perfect Israelites.

<summary>This title page introduces Johann Rudolf Glauber's alchemical treatise, which claims that the "Golden Fleece" or the secret of gold-making is hidden in plain sight within common materials like sand and stones. It positions the work as a charitable revelation intended to help the poor and oppressed by teaching them how to provide for their families through chemical knowledge.</summary>

### THE
## EPISTLE Of W.C. <note>original: "VV.C." Likely William Cooper, a prominent 17th-century publisher of alchemical texts.</note>
### TO THE
### CHRISTIAN AND COURTEOUS

God, who created man from earth or clay and who could raise up descendants for Abraham out of stones, has sent you Manna <note>A miraculous food provided by God to the Israelites in the desert; here used as a metaphor for spiritual or physical nourishment found in alchemy.</note> here, and has commanded these very stones to provide you with bread.

For everything you touch by this Art <term>The "Art" refers to Alchemy, specifically the "Great Work" of transmuting base metals into gold or seeking spiritual perfection.</term> may turn to gold, and purchase whatever you need for yourself, friends, and family, without borrowing, extortion, or fear of want, or wearing longer ears <note>A reference to the donkey ears of Midas, symbolizing foolishness or greed.</note> than will suit a rational man and a good Christian. 

And so you may prove to be a true *Fortunatus* <note>A legendary figure in European folklore who possessed an inexhaustible purse.</note>, or a providential *Midas*, and procure for yourself a lighter heart than many who have a heavier purse—which may be exhausted, lost, or spent on their lusts, and yet not satisfy their fears or covetous desires, even in a present plenty of corn and wine.
