<meta>This page continues the "Epistle to the Reader" by William Cooper. It follows a discussion on how the alchemical "Art" can provide wealth and security, transitioning here into the spiritual and social responsibilities of the alchemist.</meta>

### To the Reader.

...in a small quantity, and you may gain the Philosopher's Stone <term>A legendary substance in alchemy capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</term>, and moreover make gold more plentiful than in Solomon's <note>Solomon: The Biblical King of Israel renowned for his immense wealth and wisdom.</note> days. You may ride in triumph over the world on this "Golden Ass" <note>A reference to *The Golden Ass* (or *Metamorphoses*) by Apuleius, used here metaphorically to describe the path to wealth through alchemy.</note> by [Johann Rudolf] Glauber's <note>Glauber (1604–1670) was a famous German-Dutch chemist and alchemist who discovered many chemical processes; his "Chemical Light" refers to his influential published works.</note> new chemical light, without having old Balaam's <note>Balaam: A biblical figure whose donkey was given the miraculous power of speech to warn him; the author suggests gaining the "Ass" (the wealth) without the donkey's stubborn nature.</note> nature. *To what do you not drive mortal hearts, you cursed hunger for gold!* <note>original: "Quid non Mortalia pectora cogis Auri, facra fames." A famous line from Virgil’s *Aeneid* (3.56-57), lamenting the destructive power of greed.</note>

Therefore, let this Art breed in you a holy hunger for God, rather than for gold. Improve this talent for the honor of God who sent it, and for the good of your honest neighbors. Do not fear becoming poorer, even though you light your neighbor's candle by sharing something of this Art—or its fruits—generously, just as you would want others to do for you. 

In this way, everyone may glorify the Almighty Giver for His great treasures and bounty, and live together in peace and love, without greed <note>original: "Griping"</note>, resentment, or anxiety. From this may spring the true Golden Age <term>A mythological period of primordial peace, harmony, and prosperity, which alchemists believed could be restored through their work.</term>, so long expected and

*...Do not judge these things rashly when you do not know them; but first prove and try them thoroughly. Although you might fail (as it may easily happen to the inexperienced), do not blame my writings or my good intentions, but rather your own lack of ability or inexperience. For I write nothing here except what I have often achieved, and can perform and prove true at any time.* 

*Therefore, first consult with other more experienced seekers <note>original: "searchers"; those investigating the secrets of nature or alchemy.</note>, who I hope have not all failed and lost their labor in such an easy work—one that even a boy of ten years old may understand to be possible and feasible <note>original: "Fecible".</note>.*

*Nevertheless, do not believe that I would set down here the method for extracting gold in lumps or great quantities for extravagant use <note>original: "profuse usage".</note>; instead, I shall take care and avoid doing that.*

-># Epistle to the Reader.<-

<sig>G 3</sig>

N. B. <note>Abbreviation for the Latin *nota bene*, meaning "note well."</note> Now, as I have said, throughout all parts of the world and in every sort of sand, pebbles, and stones, good gold is contained—excepting limestones, which alone seldom or never have any gold. Otherwise, in all rocks of gritty <note>original: "Greety"</note> sand and flints of whatever color; also in gravel, surface crust <note>original: "Scurfe"; likely referring to mineral deposits or slag-like crusts</note>, or ballast on mountains and in valleys; deep within the earth <note>original: "in the Bowels of the Earth"</note>, in the sea, ponds, pits, rivers, and floods (none at all excepted), there is gold to be found. 

However, sand and stones hold the most in hot countries; and although they may be white, clear, and shining, without the least color, yet there is still some gold. Yes, it is even found sometimes in clay grounds, and in man-made <note>original: "Artificial"</note> baked tiles and bricks.

<summary>Glauber asserts that gold is a nearly universal element found in almost all types of earth, stone, and water, with the exception of limestone. He notes that while it is most abundant in hot climates, it even exists in processed materials like bricks and tiles.</summary>

# The First Kind
# of Proof

Take white sand or flints, in which you think there is not the least bit of gold, to which add three parts of *minium* <note>original: "Minium"; red lead oxide</note>, or any other powder or *calx* <note>original: "Calx"; the powdery substance or "ash" left after a metal has been roasted or burned</note> of lead. Melt <note>original: "Flux"</note> this mixture in a covered crucible <note>original: "Crufible"</note> in a wind furnace, or by the blast of bellows, and let them melt well together for one hour. It will turn into a yellow glass; then, pour it out, lest by delay it pierce the crucible and run among the ashes. 

Powder this glass and mix with it half its weight of *alkali salt* <note>original: "Sal Alcali"; a basic salt such as potash or soda used as a flux</note>, soap, or potash. Then, put this mixture into an iron pot or crucible, where you have first placed nails or other bits of iron. Melt <note>original: "Flux"</note> this in the fire, and the lead glass will be reduced into a solid metal <note>original: "body"</note> again by the iron. Pour this out into an ingot mold or cone, and the *regulus* <note>original: "Regulus"; the pure metallic mass that sinks to the bottom of a crucible during smelting</note> of lead will sink to the bottom, while the flints or sand (like slag <note>original: "Scurffe"</note> and dross) will 

<summary>Glauber provides a step-by-step metallurgical procedure using lead oxide and iron to extract trace amounts of gold from seemingly "empty" white sand or flint. He describes the process of turning minerals into glass and then reducing them back into a metallic state to isolate the gold-bearing lead.</summary>

<keywords>metallurgy, assaying, gold extraction, minium, crucible, fluxing, regulus, alchemy, John Rudolf Glauber</keywords>

...swim on the top, but the lead will contract such a black roughness that it will not flow easily. For this, use the following remedy: Place this *regulus* <note>original: "Regulus"; the metallic mass that sinks to the bottom of a crucible during smelting</note> in a wind furnace. For every one ounce of the melted *regulus*, add a dram <note>a unit of weight, approximately 3.9 grams</note> or slightly more of *saltpeter* <note>original: "Salt Peter"; potassium nitrate, used here as a purifying flux</note>, and let them melt together. Then the *saltpeter* <note>original: "Sal Nitre"</note> will draw the black roughness from the lead into a slag <note>original: "Scurffe"</note>. Once this is poured out and melted again, it becomes workable <note>original: "tractable"</note> and white, and will easily flow upon a cupel <note>original: "Test"; a shallow, porous dish used in assaying</note>.

But if you do not have the skill to perform this work, put your black, rough *regulus* of lead into a crucible or cupel of the type commonly called a *treibscerbe* <note>original: "Treibscerbe"; a German term for a refining dish or large, shallow crucible bottom</note> (which is like a large, hard crucible bottom). Cover it and let it purge itself in the fire for half an hour, or at least for fifteen minutes, and it will become white and workable. However, the washing or cleansing with *saltpeter* is much better. Weigh out a pennyweight <note>a small unit of mass in the troy system</note>, a dram, or a scruple of this, and an equal quantity of common lead. Test them separately in a hard, fixed <term>cupel</term> <note>a shallow, porous vessel used to separate precious metals from lead by heating</note>. This *regulus* will contain a grain of gold, while the common lead will contain only a grain of silver.

### *The Third Method or Manner of Proof*

Dissolve <term>♄</term> <note>the alchemical symbol for Saturn, representing lead</note> or lead in *aqua fortis* <note>original: "Aqua fortis"; literally "strong water," refers to nitric acid</note>, and pour it into salt water. Mix three parts of this *calx* with one part of powdered flints or sand, and add half as much salt derived from lees <note>alkaline salts extracted from the sediment of liquids like wine or lye</note> or another alkali. 

Mix them and put them into an iron crucible containing old nails or bits of iron. Fill it to the top and cover it tightly for half an hour to melt and flow, until all the sharp, corrosive spirits in the lead are neutralized <note>original: "mortified"</note> by the iron. Then the lead will be reduced to a solid body as before. Cast this into a tapered ingot or cone, and the *regulus* <note>the metallic mass that sinks to the bottom of a crucible during smelting</note> of lead will sink to the bottom. This must be washed and cleansed with saltpeter <note>original: "Salt Peter"; potassium nitrate</note>, or in a fixed cupel under a tile, until it purges out the dross or waste <note>original: "fæces"</note>. ## *The true way to test all flints, rocky stones, pebbles, and sands, etc., legitimately and infallibly, to see whether they contain much or little gold; with a clear explanation for everything.*

Take four ounces of sand, flints, or other stones. Anneal <note>original: "neal"; to heat a material and allow it to cool slowly to make it less brittle or easier to grind</note> them until they are red hot in a crucible, then quench them in cold water; in this way, they become workable enough to be beaten or ground into a powder. Put these four ounces of powder into a glass cucurbit <note>original: "Cucurbit"; a gourd-shaped chemical flask used in distillation</note> or a retort <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck used for distilling or decomposing substances</note>, and pour two ounces of *aqua regia* <note>original: "Aqua Regis"; Latin for "Royal Water," a highly corrosive mixture of acids—usually nitric and hydrochloric—capable of dissolving gold</note> over it to moisten the powder very well and thoroughly. Let it sit in a warm sand bath for half an hour, and the *aqua regia* will extract all the gold out of the flints or sand.

To this, pour in two ounces of warm water and stir it very well. Then strain or filter it through filter paper <note>original: "Cap Paper"; a type of paper used at the time for filtering liquids</note>; the water will pass through the paper along with the tincture <note>original: "Tincture"; a solution containing the extracted medicinal or metallic essence</note>, leaving the sand behind in the paper. Then pour more warm water into the paper and let it run through the sand again; this will wash away all the remaining gold and tincture from the sand and carry it into the receiver <note>a vessel used to collect the results of a distillation or filtration</note>, where it is to be added to the rest of the liquid. Then pour onto this impregnated <note>saturated or soaked with a substance</note> water or liquor some ordinary lees <note>the sediment or dregs of a liquid, such as wine, often used as a source of alkaline salts</note> or rather

## The third kind or manner of Proof <note>original: "Proof" here refers to an assay or chemical test to verify the presence of metal</note>.

Then the lead will be reduced to a solid body as before. ## The true manner of testing <note>original: "proving"; in this context, an assay or chemical trial</note> all *flints*, *rocky stones*, *pebbles*, and *sands*, etc., *legitimately* and *infallibly*, whether they contain much *gold* or *little*; *with a plain reason for all*.

Take four ounces of sand, flints, or other stones. Anneal <note>original: "neal"; to heat a material and then cool it, often to make it less brittle or to change its properties</note> them until they are red-hot in a crucible, and quench them in cold water. By doing this, they become easy to handle <note>original: "tractable"</note> so that they may be beaten or ground to powder. 

Put these four ounces of powder into a glass cucurbit <note>a gourd-shaped vessel used in alchemy and chemistry for distillation</note> or retort <note>a glass vessel with a long, downward-pointing neck, used for heating substances or distilling</note>, and pour two ounces of *aqua regia* <note>original: "Aqua Regis"; Latin for "royal water," a powerful mixture of nitric and hydrochloric acids capable of dissolving gold</note> over them to moisten the powders very well and thoroughly. Let it sit in warm sand for half an hour, and the *aqua regia* will extract all the gold out of the flints or sand. 

To this, pour on two ounces of warm water and stir it very well. Then, strain or filter it through filter paper <note>original: "Cap Paper"</note>; the water will pass through the paper with the tincture <note>the liquid solution containing the dissolved gold</note> and leave the sand alone in the paper. Pour more warm water onto the paper and let it run through the sand again; this will wash away all the remaining gold and tincture out of the sand and carry it into the receiver <note>the vessel that collects the filtered liquid</note>, which is likewise to be added to the rest.

### How Sand, Flints, and Similar Infused Stones May Be Identified, and Whether They Contain Little or Much Gold.

White flints, sand, and stones of all kinds contain the smallest amount of gold. Yet, they are never without some <term>volatile</term> <note>original: "Volatil"; gold in a vaporous or non-solid state that is difficult to capture or "fix"</note> essence, though it cannot be extracted profitably. Most commonly, yellow and red stones contain the most gold, yet they do not always contain enough to cover the <term>charge</term> <note>original: "charge"; the financial cost or expense of the refining process</note> of dissolving and extracting it.

Yellow, dark, and black stones commonly contain a great deal of gold. This is also true of yellow sand and stones where yellow lines are found running through white material like veins—especially if they shine clearly and glisten with many small sparks of <term>gold</term> <note>represented in the original by the alchemical symbol ☉</note> close together.

Likewise, sand that appears like <term>talc</term> <note>original: "Talc"; a shimmering mineral that alchemists often associated with the "seed" or "sulfur" of metals</note> is rich in gold. In this sand, some stones are found in which red or dark talc appears; indeed, gold is found in all talc, though more in some and less in others.

All flints and stones found in brooks, called *Bartenston*, are sufficiently rich. Although they appear white on the outside, after they are made red-hot in a fire and broken in water, they appear as yellow as gold.

Green, yellow, or sky-colored stones, <term>translucent</term> <note>original: "translucid"; semi-transparent or clear</note>

All *quartz*, the stone found in *quarries* and the rock covering *mines*, and also *sapphire stones* and others found in *veins* in the earth like *metals* (whether exposed to air or water), contain *gold* if they are colored.

The *bloodstone*, and its relatives—*emery*, *garnets* <note>original: "Granats"</note>, and *lapis lazuli*—all contain *gold*.

*Garnets* contain both <term>corporeal gold</term> <note>physical, solid gold as opposed to its spiritual or vaporous essence</note> and the "first essence" of gold. Some contain much more than others, and some only a little. However, these stones are so hard that <term>strong waters</term> (such as *aqua fortis* <note>original: "Aqua Fort"; nitric acid, a powerful corrosive</note>) cannot dissolve them; yet a remedy may be found to extract the gold from them.

If you heat these until they are red-hot, they will give off green, yellow, or red fumes, and a snow-white color will remain on the stones. Now, if anyone knows how to capture those flying fumes, he may use them to <term>coagulate</term> <note>to turn a liquid or vapor into a solid</note> *mercury* into *gold*.

Likewise, by means of *distillation*, a *green water* can be drawn out of all such stones; in this water, *mercury* will solidify into *gold*. This *green water* is what the ancients called their *green*...

If anyone gains any benefit from this, let them give praise to God and be mindful of the poor. If not, let them believe they are not yet worthy to have such things shared with them; for truly, I have written here as clearly and truthfully as no **philosopher** <note>original: "Philosopher"; here meaning an alchemist or "natural philosopher" who studies the secrets of nature</note> ever did before me.

<meta>This page continues the author's description of a new and revolutionary method for extracting gold from common materials like sand and stone, following his warnings about the secrecy of the art.</meta>

...extracted from sand, etc., and in a way that was never known to the world before.

1. My first method uses a liquid that is very inexpensive <note>original: "of small charge or price"</note>, which may be obtained in large quantities without the need for distillation.

2. My second method involves a unique metal from which cauldrons may be made. In these vessels, the stones and sand are boiled with the inexpensive liquid mentioned above, yet the metal is not corroded or consumed by the process. After the liquid dissolves any gold out of the sand or stones, you may draw out the sand and liquid with a scoop or bowl designed for this purpose, which has holes in the bottom and a wooden basket strainer placed over them. In this way, the saturated liquid or **solvent** <term>original: *Menstruum*; an alchemical term for a substance that dissolves a solid</term> containing the gold can pass through, leaving the sand or stones behind in the scoop or bowl. Then, pour more warm water onto the sand to wash out any remaining gold and **essence** <term>original: "Tincture"; here referring to the dissolved gold or its concentrated qualities</term>. After everything is washed out, throw the sand or stones away as useless.

3. My third shortcut is to pour another unique and inexpensive type of liquid into the clear solvent that contains the gold or essence. This causes all the gold and essence (at a specific concentration and quantity) within the solvent to settle to the bottom through **precipitation** <term>the process of a substance separating from a liquid solution into a solid form</term>. The clear solvent, now freed from the gold, must be poured off to be used again for the same purpose. It retains its full strength and effectiveness without any weakening or loss, whether from the precipitating liquid or any other cause. If any of the solvent is lost or spilled during use, it can be easily replaced by obtaining more of the same without much trouble or expense.

<summary>The author outlines a three-step chemical process for extracting gold from sand and stones using inexpensive solvents, specialized metal cauldrons, and a recycling method for the chemical agents.</summary>

( 49 )

Now, if anyone should mix any precipitating **alkaline** <term>original: *Lixiviat*; a liquid produced by leaching ashes, such as lye</term> liquid or dregs with the aforementioned solvent, contrary to its nature, they would thereby **neutralize** <note>original: "mortifie"</note> the solvent by precipitating the gold. This is done in other processes and was used in my former experiments and trials in this book concerning white sand and stones, etc. However, consider what damage and loss would result from that: every time there is an occasion to use it, our solvent would be destroyed, and the extraction would thereby become very troublesome and expensive <note>original: "chargeable"</note>, especially when done in glass or earthen **cucurbits** <term>gourd-shaped laboratory vessels used for distillation</term> or containers. 

But by this new method, everything costs almost nothing and may be conducted in larger vessels and more cheaply, and the aforementioned waters may be used without loss. This kind of extraction may be compared to the making of **saltpeter** <note>original: *Salt-Peter*; potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in gunpowder</note>, where the workman, having extracted the saltpeter, throws away all the ashes and dirt and puts more material into the **vats** <note>original: *Cupam*</note> (tubs or bowls) for the same common water to extract more.

1. Our fourth **shortcut** <term>original: "Compendium"; here meaning an efficient or summarized method</term> is that the precipitated **powder** <term>original: "Calx"; a powdery metallic oxide or substance produced by precipitation</term> of gold, after being filtered in a bag, is taken out and dried. By using a good, cheap, and uniquely effective substance to **melt** <term>original: "flux"; to treat a metal with a substance to promote melting and purification</term> it, it is reduced to a solid metal body; in this way, no part of the gold will be lost or diminished.

Through these four shortcuts for the extraction of gold, profit will be gained, but not so much by other methods.

Now, let no one marvel why I do not reveal any of these four shortcuts here; I have been "bitten" enough by the envy of other men. Where they could not understand my writings due to their own dullness—even though I had expressed the matter plainly enough—and so could not perform the work, they then publicly brought a scandal upon me. They reported that whatever I wrote...

<summary>The author explains that his method allows for the reuse of chemical solvents, making gold extraction far cheaper than traditional alchemy, and defends his decision to keep specific details secret due to past professional slander.</summary>
<keywords>gold extraction, solvent recovery, lixiviat, saltpeter, alchemy, metallurgical flux, secrecy, chemical vessels</keywords>

...it were lies; No, some others have seen the thing performed, and yet afterwards, out of hatred and envy, have slighted it and me.

But however, while I live (by God's Grace and Providence), I shall be helpful to my neighbor by using my talent to serve them. Like a most bright, shining light, I will show the wonderful, great mystery of God to ignorant and simple people, against the will of all the enemies of Truth. Though they fret and vex ever so much at it, I have resolved to do so; Yes, behold! Though my adversaries should all conspire and wholly devour me alive, they should swallow but a meager or lean morsel of earth; for ***Glauber*** <note>Johann Rudolf Glauber (1604–1670) was a famous German-Dutch alchemist and chemist, known for his work on salts and acids.</note> would be and remain ***Glauber*** still, until the end <note>original: "consummation"</note> of the world or ages. Now, if these men were of the ancient character and framework of faith and virtue, they would not disparage and scandalize their innocent neighbor without him having deserved ill at their hands.

Let these things be sufficient for now concerning the extraction of gold out of sand, stones, and flints.

Now, furthermore I say: although everyone might use this extraction of gold for their employment or trade, yet one person would not be a hindrance to the profit of another. This is because stones and sand are available <note>original: "obvious"</note> to everyone in all countries, and the salts that are useful for extracting the metal are also plentiful; so nothing is wanting but a lover of the work to set his hand to it.

***Paracelsus*** <note>Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim (1493–1541) was a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of minerals and chemicals in medicine.</note>, in his book *The Vexations of Alchemists*, says that more gold and silver is found upon the earth than in its depths <note>original: "bowels thereof"</note>. It is most certainly true, and will remain true; for a lie cannot degrade itself or raise itself to become a truth. In due time, it shall be punished in eternal darkness with the Devil (as the father and origin of all liars). Without doubt, the laughter of Democritus <note>Democritus (c. 460 – c. 370 BC) was a Greek philosopher known as the "laughing philosopher" because he mocked human follies.</note> and the weeping of Heraclitus <note>Heraclitus (c. 535 – c. 475 BC) was known as the "weeping philosopher" because of his melancholy view of human life.</note> came from their observation of mortal man's eager pursuit of gold and silver through great anxieties, labors, and troubles. Men lose their health and risk their souls and bodies, often sailing across the vast ocean for it, or tearing open the earth to rush and sink down into it to fetch out gold and silver. Yet, these metals are so plentiful and easy to find upon the surface <note>original: "superficies"</note> of the earth in every region and country that a sufficient amount <note>original Greek: αὐτάρκεια (autarkeia)</note> may be had.

Solomon did not write without purpose when he said that great power <note>original: "virtue"</note> was in herbs, wood, and stones. For that which is fixed in stones is volatile in herbs, as I demonstrated in my little treatise printed in 1663. The first essence <note>original Latin: Ens</note> of gold (from which gold may be made) is present in both.

We also read in the book of Esdras <note>Esdras is a name used for books of the Apocrypha associated with the prophet Ezra.</note> that there is much earth used for vessels or pots, but only a little powder or dust to make gold. Not all sorts of earth are rich enough to profit from the extraction of gold, nor should it be thought that every stone and every grain of sand is so rich in physical gold <note>original: "Corporal Gold"</note> as to yield any profit. Yet, they still contain the first essence <note>original Latin: Ens</note> of gold, or yield such a <term>Calx</term> <note>original: Calx; a powder produced by the thorough heating/roasting of a mineral or metal</note>, by which (or with its help) good gold may be made. If we knew how to extract and prepare this <term>Calx</term> or powder, we would place greater value on it and esteem it more than gold itself. Now, although such a <term>gold-making</term> <note>original: Aurifying</note> powder is so widely distributed and diffused in stones and sand, etc., it is still not easy to beat it or force

One cannot force it out with a hammer; rather, it must be extracted and perfected only by a specialized art. Consequently, the blind multitude of greedy gold-hunters will no more believe it than those who are ignorant of the art. Yet, this art has always been esteemed among philosophers as their greatest "Secret of Secrets," and so it has been preserved among them.

Also, where *Paracelsus* <note>Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a Swiss physician and alchemist who pioneered the use of chemicals and minerals in medicine.</note> writes of the "first essence" <note>original Latin: *Ens*</note> of gold, he tells us it may be drawn forth by sublimation <note>The process of heating a substance until it turns into vapor and then cooling it back into a solid.</note>. *Basilius Valentinus* <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist whose writings were influential in the development of chemistry.</note> also tells us that the preparation of the Universal Tincture <note>A potent alchemical substance, often equated with the Philosopher's Stone, used for transmutation or healing.</note> may be compared to the distillation or extraction of the "burning spirit of wine" <note>An early term for concentrated alcohol or brandy.</note> from the dregs, and may be obtained in that way. 

Oh friends, this is truly a sufficiently clear comparison! Just as a small amount of good spirit is hidden within a great quantity of wine or beer dregs, and the residue is merely useless mud, yet that little quantity of spirit is drawn out profitably by means of distillation. It is thereby concentrated into a small space, and is such a potent <note>original: "virtuous"</note> and piercing spirit that one spoonful of it is worth more than a whole cask <note>original: "Runlet"</note> or vessel full of dregs <note>original Latin: *fæces*</note>. 

In such ways or means, the philosophers would have us draw forth and extract the first essence <note>original Latin: *Primum Ens*</note> or "Form" of gold by art out of stones and sand—even though it is dispersed and diffused widely within them. By concentrating their power and Tincture into a small compass, a very small quantity (even if only as big as a pea) is of more worth and value than a great mountain of useless and unprofitable dead earth.

Further, I would not conceal this from you: throughout all of Germany, in and near the rivers, stones are found which abound with gold and silver...

...vessel ever, and are sufficiently rich. When this is melted with borax, it will yield a silvery gold. I must solemnly declare that I never saw or knew any mortal man who understood or observed those stones before, much less the golden powder hidden in them. This neglect is undoubtedly due to people's carelessness in seeking out the great natural <note>original: "Physical," referring here to the study of the physical world or nature</note> mysteries of God.

Now, I must advise everyone that it is of great importance for parents to train their children in their youth with some honest artisan or craftsman. This would teach them a skill that, in case of necessity, might earn them an honest and commendable livelihood. The rich, having a plentiful estate, think they will leave enough for their children to never want for anything. Yet, if one misfortune or another happens to them or their children—such as house fires, shipwrecks, goods lost to pirates or thieves, creditors failing, or shipping disasters—they do not know where to turn or what course to take. They have no choice but to flee, live like vagabonds, or rot in jail <note>original: "Gaol"</note>; all this for lack of some praiseworthy skill learned in their youth.

Because of this, they become desperate. One man abandons his wife and children to travel to the *Indies* <note>In the 17th century, the "Indies" referred generally to the East Indies (South and Southeast Asia) or the West Indies (the Caribbean), often viewed as places of both great wealth and extreme danger.</note>, where many are devoured by beasts or cannibals, some are drowned or starved, and others sell themselves into servitude or become soldiers and are eventually slain like mad dogs. Others, after they have spent their wealth and can no longer support their families, begin to live immoral lives and have a miserable, sorrowful life until they perish and go to hell. All of this might have been avoided by learning some good mechanical arts <term>The "mechanical arts" were practical or manual trades, such as metalworking, weaving, or carpentry, often contrasted with the "liberal arts" like logic or rhetoric.</term> in

...in their youth, or prosperous circumstances. But when difficult and violent times approach, or when too many people work the same trade in a city, they impoverish one another. In such cases, there is no remedy but medicine <note>original: "physick"</note>, which may also fail. 

But a physician <note>original: "Physitian"</note> might learn another skill to earn a livelihood besides his medical practice. Then he would not need to make so many visits just to crave <note>original: "gape for"</note> fees from his poor, distressed patients. Similarly, the lawyer would not need to sell the law or their client’s case for base profit, preparing himself a seat in hell to dwell in forever. Nor would the clergyman <note>original: "Divine"</note> need to fear his patrons or benefactors and soothe them in their sins. Instead, he could preach the truth to everyone without flattery, preferring God’s honor and the people’s real well-being—with true zeal—over his own private profit, which puts his soul at risk. This applies to all other professions as well.

Now, having addressed or touched upon this matter, I depart sighing and mourning that *genuine Hermetic philosophy and medicine* <note>Hermetic philosophy refers to a tradition of knowledge—often involving alchemy and spiritual wisdom—attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note> are so little practiced or esteemed. The same is true for natural, true *alchemy* (and not the corrupted kind), which is a genuine art and the *queen of all arts*, and shall remain so until the end of the world.

Therefore, since this art of extracting wealth from sand and stones is such a great and useful treasure as we have heard—yet is carelessly kicked aside by men everywhere—why do we not instead extract them to support ourselves and our families? This would defend us against the hardships of the times in a handsome and honest way. Why, I ask, do we not leave the *Indies* <note>In the 17th century, the "Indies" referred generally to the East Indies (South and Southeast Asia) or the West Indies (the Caribbean), often viewed as places of both great wealth and extreme danger.</note> to their own inhabitants and manage our own countries or the earth in *Europe* where we live? There is more than enough here to sustain us for whatever we need. I cannot help but sincerely <note>original: "ingeniously," meaning frankly or honestly in this context</note> confess again and again that...

If it were possible to renew my youth, or call back just ten years, I would not neglect to publicly profess and teach true philosophy, medicine, and alchemy, making them known through demonstration <note>original: "demonstratively"</note>. But the sand in my hourglass has almost run out <note>The "sand of my glass" is a common metaphor for the remaining time in one's life.</note>, and my time is short. Therefore, I cannot undertake such laborious practices myself, but must leave and resign them to others who are in the prime of their youth and strength, while I am fading and departing from this world. 

But all the good I can do while I live through faithful writing, I shall not neglect for my neighbor's profit and advantage. And, God favoring my purpose, I shall shortly publish secrets never heard of before. For now, it only remains to bring this treatise <note>original: "Tractate"</note> to an end.

<page-num>(56)</page-num>

## An Admonition to the Kind <note>original: "Courteous"</note> Reader.

**W**hatever I have written in this little book about extracting gold from sand, stones, and flints is so true and certain that there should be no question about it. However, I must tell you that as soon as this treatise went to press, I thought of another way to extract gold from stones that is far better than the previous one. By this method, gold may be drawn out and extracted much faster and more effectively; because in this new way, there is no need at all for kettles made of copper or brass, etc. Instead, great quantities may be extracted without boiling in such vessels, using others that are available everywhere. Consequently, one man using this new method can easily extract the gold from a thousand (1,000 <note>original Roman numeral: "CIƆ"</note>) pounds of sand or stones in a single day.

Therefore, I cannot help but communicate this method as well (which is far superior to the former) if I understand that it will be generally profitable and gratefully accepted during these difficult times and the fear of worse to come. In this way, I hope to be of public service to my country and to future generations. And so, I commit all things to the guidance and protection of the Almighty.

->Dated at Amsterdam, July 15/26,<-
->In the year of our Lord 1666.<-
<note>The dual date reflects the concurrent use of the Julian and Gregorian calendars in the 17th century.</note>

<summary>In this postscript, the author announces he has discovered a newer, more efficient method for extracting gold that requires no specialized metal kettles and allows one person to process 1,000 pounds of material a day.</summary>
<keywords>gold extraction, metallurgy, Amsterdam, innovation, chemistry, 1666, laboratory equipment</keywords>

# Epistle to the Reader.

...metaphysical and Physical Figure <note>original: "physical and Physical Figure"; the catchword from the previous page was "physical," completing the term "metaphysical"</note>, along with my smaller *Philosophical Epitaph* and Figures, I thought it good to combine them with the rest into one small volume. *It is God’s greatest bounty to give light and eyes to see, not only the physical and temporary things, but the spiritual and eternal light of wisdom.*

**As much light as anything has, so much it has of the divine.** <note>original Latin: "Quantum quidque habet Luminis, tantum et numinis."</note> *The more* **Light** *someone has, the more they have of God, who dwells in* **Light**, *and in his children, who are children of Light and Life. For this is the condemnation and death: that* **Light** *has come into the world, and men love darkness rather than light, because their deeds are evil.* <note>This is a paraphrase of John 3:19.</note> *Therefore, let this serve as a trumpet in these latter days to awaken and teach men what God, the world, and devils are, so that their souls...*

Many people talk of *Heaven* and of being in its *Glory* with *Christ*, yet they do not have it within them; nor do they truly desire to be there with the disciplined <term>mortified</term> <note>In a religious context, "mortified" refers to the practice of subduing one's physical desires and worldly pride through self-discipline and spiritual devotion.</note>, pure, and peaceable *Company* that goes there. To better distinguish and reflect upon the way and the *Company* suited for *Heaven*, consider these four *Observations*: 

*   To do *Evil* for *good* is *devilish*.
*   To do *Evil* for *Evil* is *Natural*, *Sensual*, and *Bestial*.
*   To do *Good* for *Good* is *Human* <note>original: "Humane"</note>.
*   To do *Good* for *Evil* is *Divine*.

Therefore, the *Wisdom* from above is always *Pure*, *Holy*, and *Good*. # Epistle to the Reader.

...and consider him still in his nature <note>original: "property"; referring here to God's inherent qualities or attributes.</note> and works to be *Infinite, Wise, Omnipotent,* and *Omniscient* (just as well as merciful). Then, following *Christ’s example*, they would avoid all occasions and the appearance of sin, just as they are able and willing to do in certain *acts* simply because a *child* is present.

They would also believe that he who made and *created* the *eye* and *ear*, and gives them *life* and *sense* <note>original: "Senfe"; meaning the faculty of perception or feeling.</note> at the very moment they are used, can both see and hear as well as any *eye* or *ear*. Indeed, no eye or ear can see or hear anything at any time without his *help*.

Likewise, they would believe that he is just as truly present (though *invisible to our outward senses*) as any *creature* he has made. ## TO THE
## Lovers of Wisdom.

*Dear Readers,*

We remember and know that all understanding and wisdom come from God, and we receive all good things from the Father of Lights <note>original: "Father of Lights"; a biblical title for God found in James 1:17, referring to the Creator of the sun, moon, and stars.</note>. Wisdom is nothing else but the breathing of God, who sends his Spirit and teaches men what wisdom is: the truth and true knowledge. This knowledge consists chiefly in three things: 
1. To know God. 
2. To know ourselves. 
3. To know that which God has created.

Following wisdom and knowledge comes judgment; specifically, to distinguish good from evil, light from darkness, and truth from falsehood. Judgment and understanding are followed by choice <note>original: "Election"; in this context, the theological and philosophical capacity to choose between two paths.</note> and the will to do the one and to avoid the other.

The knowledge or understanding of all things is threefold: 
1. Of Men. 
2. Of Angels. 
3. Of God. 

Human understanding (or knowledge) is only partial. The knowledge of angels is held in fear and trembling; but God’s knowledge alone is perfect.

# The Preface.

Lastly, the Spirit of God’s holy ones, who, being godly and spiritually minded, are taught by God.

The wisdom and knowledge of the first type of men is full of folly, darkness, and ignorance. The wisdom of the second is full of misleading philosophy and continual contentions. The wisdom of the third sort of men, who are godly, is only in part, although it is true and good (*Romans 1:29; Ephesians 4:18; Colossians 2:8; 2 Timothy 3:4; 1 Corinthians 13:9, 11*).

Truly wise men dive into the best gifts and perfection, which are of three sorts: charity, prophecy, and examination <note>original: "Examination"; here meaning the spiritual gift of discernment or the ability to test and judge the truth of spirits and doctrines.</note>.

Love and charity are the center and encompass the circle of all godly virtues, and they possess faith and hope; but prophesying has all knowledge, wisdom, and doctrine. Lastly, examination contains all understanding, judgment, and discretion. In

# The Preface.

In these three things, everything is contained that belongs to wisdom, the center of which is the word of God. This is what all people ought to study and should communicate to others according to the gift they have received from the Spirit of grace. This is done so that God, the author of all good, may be glorified, and so that no one may boast of their gifts or exalt themselves above others, but rather remain humble. 

Furthermore, no one should quench the Spirit, neither in themselves nor in others, but should instead stir it up. And lastly, let no one despise prophecy, so that he may not offend God or his neighbor, nor bring disgrace <note>original: "scandalize"</note> upon himself. 

Love endures all things; the wisdom of the spirit searches all things; and examination <note>original: "Examination"; referring to the spiritual gift of discernment or testing the truth of a spirit or doctrine.</note> tests all things. 

Since we have undertaken, through the prompting of the spirit, to speak of wisdom (as far as our limited knowledge allows), therefore...

# *The Preface.*

We earnestly ask the reader in love that those whom we displease (or who are offended) would tolerate us in love, knowing that wise men must also bear with fools. Let the things spoken of in this book not be immediately rejected, but rather be allowed to stand, remembering that God is also patient toward sinners. 

But if anyone thinks himself wise, let him show the spirit of judgment <note>original: "Judgment"; here referring to the spiritual gift of discernment or the ability to weigh the truth of a matter.</note>, and let him discern carefully, lest he also judge himself. For we believe that we have also received a gift of the Spirit of grace, which we will not allow to die; instead, to the praise of the Lord, we will put it to use out of love for the children of wisdom. We do this not as a formal instruction, but as a good testimony to ourselves that we have not received the gift of the Spirit in vain.

...hope to be beneficial to the children of wisdom. We may have published something similar twelve years ago, titled *The Dawn of Wisdom* <note>original: *Aurora Sapientiæ*</note>; yet since it has been requested by some again, I have not changed the title, hoping that it is significantly <note>original: "not a little"; an early modern idiom meaning "very much" or "considerably."</note> improved and corrected. I have presented it briefly, so that it may be neither tedious for the reader, nor expensive <note>original: "chargeable"</note> for the buyer, nor yet laborious <note>original: "painful"</note> for the printer. Kind reader, please accept all of this in good spirit, and thus we entrust all well-wishers to God's gracious protection.

## THE DAWN OF WISDOM: <note>original Latin: "AURORA SAPIENTIÆ"</note>
# Morning Light,
->OR<-
# Dawning of Wisdom.
---

We take the liberty, according to the gift of the Spirit, *to speak briefly of Wisdom in this short treatise without being overly wordy <note>original: "prolixity"</note>. And because we mentioned a threefold knowledge in the Preface—namely that of humanity, of angels, and of God—we will now explain here that wisdom is also threefold:* 1. *The natural knowledge of all created things*. 2. *The wisdom of faith leading to salvation*. And 3. *The secret and mystical wisdom, which is generally unknown. These we call* true philosophy <note>original: "vera Philosophia"</note>, theology <note>original: "Theologia"</note>, and theosophy <note>original: "Theosophia"; refers to a divine wisdom or "God-knowledge" often associated with mystical insight.</note>. We will speak of these three as briefly as possible. *May the Spirit of the Lord be upon both the writer and the reader. Amen.*

<meta>catchword: LET THERE BE LIGHT <note>original Hebrew: "JEHIOR" (יְהִי אוֹר); the command from Genesis 1:3, "Let there be light."</note></meta>

<keywords>Wisdom, Threefold Knowledge, Natural Philosophy, Theology, Theosophy, Mysticism, Spirit of the Lord, Jehior</keywords>

# JEHIOR, <note>original: "Jehior" is a transliteration of the Hebrew phrase "Yehi Or," meaning "Let there be light" (Genesis 1:3).</note>

->OR THE<-

## Morning Light of Wisdom.

### CHAPTER 1.

*Concerning the Books of Wisdom, in which wisdom may be learned; How and in what manner?*

There are primarily only three books in which all wisdom is contained. Specifically: 
1. The whole of Nature and Creation, found in the great *Book of Heaven and Earth.* 
2. The Book of Holy Scripture in the literal text <term>original: "Letter"</term> of the Holy Word of God. 
3. Humanity itself. 

The sole center or source <term>original: "principle"</term> of these three is the Word of God, which is the primary book from which these other three books have their origin.

<summary>This opening page introduces the three fundamental sources of wisdom—Nature, Scripture, and Humanity—all of which are derived from a single source: the Word of God.</summary>
<keywords>Jehior, Wisdom, Creation, Holy Scripture, Word of God, Nature, Humanity</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to the "three books" of wisdom—Nature, Holy Scripture, and Humanity—explaining how they are subdivided and what specific knowledge can be gained from each.</meta>

<header>Chapter 1. Of the Books of Wisdom, etc.</header>

The first book, Nature, contains seven other books which are the seven elements; these will be discussed in detail later.

These seven books have three other books opposite them, which are the three contrary elements; these will also be discussed later.

The second book, Holy Scripture, is divided into three other books: the Law of the Old Testament; the Gospel of the New Testament; and the Eternal Gospel of the everlasting testament and covenant, which includes the book of the *Revelation* of *Jesus Christ*.

The third book, Humanity <term>original: "the Third book of Man"</term>, is only one book. It is sealed to those who are blind but open to those who can see. In this book, all wisdom is hidden and sealed, yet also revealed and open. Mankind is called the image or honor of God (discussed further below), and humanity cannot be called by any other name (*1 Corinthians 11:7*).

From the first book, we learn philosophy <note>original: *Philosophia*</note>—the natural wisdom regarding the knowledge of created, natural things made of the elements. We learn this wisdom from the three principles and seven elements, and we distinguish them from the three contrary elements; otherwise, we cannot find the truth of natural wisdom.

From the second book, we learn theology <note>original: *Theologia*</note> or divinity—the wisdom that leads to salvation. This is found in the three aforementioned books through the seven spirits (*Isaiah 11*). We distinguish this from all human interpretations <term>original: "glosses"</term> and the writings of profane people. For the book explains itself and requires no human interpretation; it only needs faith, which grasps all things.

From the third book, which is God’s image, we learn the true knowledge of God, as well as His being

<summary>This page details the subdivisions of the three "Books of Wisdom," explaining that Nature teaches philosophy, Scripture teaches divinity, and Humanity provides the true knowledge of God's being.</summary>
<keywords>Elements, Holy Scripture, Revelation, Image of God, Philosophy, Theology, Seven Spirits, Faith</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of humanity as the "third book" of wisdom, explaining that God is best known through his image (mankind) and that true wisdom requires the guidance of the Holy Spirit.</meta>

<header>Chapter 1. Of the Books of Wisdom, etc.</header>

[...knowing his] being and essence, and his whole mystery. To such an extent, anyone who desires to know God must learn to know him in his image, and to do so perfectly. This perfect knowledge is this: that God is man, and that he who is of God is a true man, and God is in him.

This is the wisdom that has been mystical until now, yet is revealed only to the wise. It is called *Theosophy* <note>original: *Theosophia*; divine wisdom</note> because God manifests himself nowhere as clearly as in man, who is his image, honor, or glory (*1 Corinthians 11:7*). Therefore, a person does not need to go far, but only into themselves, to learn the true knowledge of God, and to seek after God within themselves and themselves in God. If one does not do this, all is in vain, and wisdom is to be found nowhere else (*Acts 17:27; Luke 17:21*).

Since the three other books proceed only from the one Book—the Word of God—all three testify unanimously <note>original: ὁμολογουμένως; in agreement or by common consent</note> of this book, namely each in its own writing, wisdom, and testimony; yet none testifies so clearly as humanity does. This is that great, complete, and perfect library of wise men, with which they may be justly and fairly satisfied.

We cannot learn all the wisdom and knowledge, along with their mysteries, in these books anywhere else except through the same Spirit who himself wrote, created, and testified to them. Therefore, whoever desires to study these books must acknowledge and confess their own blindness, folly, and ignorance from the bottom of their heart. They must pray to the Father of Lights <note>A biblical title for God from James 1:17</note> for illumination, wisdom, and understanding, asking that he would send his Holy Spirit to lead us into all truth, take us away from all foolishness and falsehood, and bring us to the light of God's glory.

This cannot be done by any other means than

We will state this: that until now all these books were sealed up, but they are now opened at the end of the world according to the prophecy in 4 Esdras 6:20 <note>4 Esdras is an apocalyptic book found in many historical Bibles, though now considered apocryphal in most traditions</note>, Daniel 12:9, and Zechariah 14:7. And if wise, worldly men are offended by this, they reveal by their reaction that the wisdom of the Holy One is not within them.

The books of wisdom testify unanimously to the Word <note>original: *Logos*; the divine reason or person of Christ as described in the Gospel of John</note> by which all things are created, and in which alone all wisdom rests. This Word is the beginning of all beginnings, in which everything exists and without which nothing exists; it is all in all, God blessed forever; Amen.

<header>Chapter 2. Of the principles and beginnings, etc.</header>

...breath or voice of God, indeed God himself in such a subsistence <note>original: "subsistence"; in historical theology, this refers to the distinct existence or "personhood" of a being, such as a person of the Trinity</note>, essence, and being. Specifically, this describes how the image of God represents us according to the likeness that is man—namely, that he is a life-giving <note>original: "quickening"</note> spirit, a spiritual *Adam*, and a heavenly man, who is God the Lord, glorified and magnified forever, *Amen.*

Now we hold completely that this is the proper definition of God, and no other, which the Holy Scripture clearly signifies (1 Corinthians 15:45, 47, 48). According to his image and likeness, he has created a spiritual *Adam* and an earthly <note>original: "terrestrial"</note> man, as when God said, "*Let us make Adam or Man after our image, after our likeness*" (Genesis 1:26, 27).

Now, since the Word is the Beginning of all Beginnings, it contains Light, Life, and Love. Light provides the *revelation of God*, for God is Light, dwells in light, and is the Father of Lights.

Life is the virtue and power of God, and a life-giving spirit who possesses, creates, and preserves all things.

Love is a testimony of God, in which exists the Father, the Son, and the Holy Spirit—all in one Word, which is called *Jesus Christ, the spiritual Adam, and heavenly Man, the Messiah <note>original: "Messias"</note>, who is essential, Alpha and Omega <note>original: "Alpha and Omega"; the first and last letters of the Greek alphabet, signifying the beginning and the end</note>, All in All, the Beginning and the End, the First and the Last, Blessed and Praised forever. Amen.* (Revelation 22:13 <note>The original text cites Revelation 1:22, but as that chapter only has 20 verses, the author likely intended Revelation 22:13 or similar passages describing Christ as the Alpha and Omega</note>).

Now, since the Word is the true Principle within God himself, then consequently everything proceeds from the Word. From the Word, three general principles primarily manifest themselves—principles in which and through which all things are contained—and they are these: God, Nature, and Element.

# Chapter 2. Of the Principles

Now these three general Principles also provide a threefold world: first, a divine uncreated world flowing from God from *Eternity*; then an *Angelical* world, which proceeds, shines, or lights forth from the Light in which God dwells; and lastly an *Elementary* world, whose origin <note>original: "Original"</note> came out of the water.

Following these three general Principles, three special Principles also proceed: namely *Spirit* <note>original: "Ghost"; in this period, "ghost" and "spirit" were often used interchangeably to describe a non-physical essence</note>, *wind*, and *Water*. Now every world has its proper *Spirit*, *Wind*, and *Water* according to their *Kind* and *Nature*. All things created out of the divine world from above are created out of *water* and *Spirit* from above, through the wind and breath of the Almighty God. For the *Spirit* is properly assigned to the Divine world; the *wind* is properly assigned to the Angelical; and to the *Elementary* world, *water* is proper.

After these special Principles follow, lastly, particular Principles, each of which has its own *Being* out of which, in which, and from which it consists. But these three Principles proceed from the former and are *Spirit*, *Soul*, and *Life*, and *Body*. All bodies are made of the *Water*; all *Life* and *Soul* come out of the *Wind*; and all *Spirit* comes out of the *Spirit*. Regarding the *Angels*, their *Body* is from the *Wind* of the *Angelical World*, their *Life* is a *fire-flame*, and their *Soul* is a *light*, which is discussed elsewhere.

These are our Principles in *Wisdom*, from which all things have their *origin*. We doubt much whether any other Principles can be shown to us.

The *First Mover* <note>original: "Primum mobile"; in medieval and early modern astronomy, the outermost sphere that moves all other celestial spheres</note> of all things is the *Word*, for in it is the *Life*.

The *Second Mover* <note>original: "Secundum mobile"</note> of all things,

<meta>is</meta>

<summary>The author outlines a hierarchical cosmology where the universe is divided into three worlds—Divine, Angelical, and Elementary—each governed by specific spiritual and physical principles derived from God's breath and the Word.</summary>
<keywords>cosmology, creation, Spirit, Angelical world, Elementary world, First Mover, Primum mobile, the Word</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion from the previous page, identifying the Spirit as the "First Mover" and completing the description of the three moving Principles—Spirit, Wind, and Water—that govern the existence of all things.</meta>

### And the beginnings of all things

...is the Spirit, through which all things are created.

The <term>third mover</term> <note>original: "Tertium mobile"</note> is the Wind, and these three moving Principles are the <term>everlasting mover</term> <note>original: "Perpetuum mobile"</note> of all things, by which all things move, live, and have their being.

But these three rest bodily upon the water, out of which the world and all things are made. They also exist in the air, according to the Life in which all things reside, and in Heaven, from which all things come from above according to the Spirit. But the Spirit comes from God; it comes from Him and returns there (John 1:4, Psalm 104:30, Acts 17:28, Ecclesiastes 12:7). Yet all of these come together on and in the Earth, as if in the heart of the world (Wisdom 1:7).

In these Principles—out of, with, and through them—all things exist. *And without these nothing can exist* that has a being. They are Light, Life, and Love; God, Nature, and Element; Spirit, Wind, and Water; Body, Soul, and Spirit—and all of that is contained within the Word.

<summary>This page identifies the Spirit, Wind, and Water as the three "moving principles" that sustain all life and motion in the universe, connecting the divine realm to the physical earth. It emphasizes that these elements coexist within the "Word" of God, forming the foundation of all created existence.</summary>

# CHAPTER III.

## *Of the first Principle of all things, which is God.*

Since God is the beginning of all beginnings—the source from which all things proceed—his own beginning comes from no other. He is without a beginning because he does not originate from another; yet, though he has his beginning from no other source, within himself he represents his own beginning to everyone.

This "beginning" of God is not a beginning for God himself, but for us; for he himself is *all in all* <note>original: "all in all" — a reference to the omnipresence and self-sufficiency of God, as seen in 1 Corinthians 15:28</note>. Now, so that he might show himself to us humans, to testify of himself and instruct us about him, God reveals himself in his beginning, which is the Word—and the Word is God himself.

Now, through this Word, it is shown, testified, and taught to us what God is and who he is. Without a word or speaking, there can be neither <term>doctrine</term> <note>teaching or body of beliefs</note>, nor testimony, nor a presentation, as Reason makes manifest. Therefore, the Word is the beginning of God *to us*, from which all testimonies, names, and relations of God proceed, as if out of the depths of God, which consist in their own proper Spirit, wind, and water.

<summary>This page explores God as the ultimate "First Principle" and explains that while God is eternal and without origin, he reveals himself to humanity through "the Word." This Word serves as the primary means of divine testimony and the source from which all knowledge of God's nature flows.</summary>

<keywords>God, First Principle, the Word, revelation, theology, Reason, Spirit, wind, water, beginning</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of God as the "First Principle," focusing on how the nature of God is unified in a single name and essence. It transitions from the general concept of the "Word" to the specific identification of that Word as Christ, who embodies the fullness of the Godhead.</meta>

# CHAPTER 3. Of the first Principle, etc.

The name of God is only one, according to his Essence <note>original: "Essence" — the intrinsic nature or indispensable quality of something that determines its character</note>, which no one knows except the one upon whom it is written with the living letters of the Spirit of God and his Word in essence, who is Christ, and those who share in his being. These are the ones in whom the Word dwells essentially, and who see his glory with a light and through an eye that no one else can see. All the names of God come together in this one name: *For the whole fullness of the Godhead dwells in him bodily, he who is called Alpha and Omega <note>original: "Alpha and Omega" — the first and last letters of the Greek alphabet, representing the beginning and the end</note>.* (Zechariah 14:9; Colossians 2:9). *He who sees him also sees God, the Father, the living Word, and the life-giving <note>original: "quickning"</note> Spirit.* (John 14:9; 1 John 1; 1 Corinthians 15:45). In the same way, someone who sees a man also sees the body, soul, and spirit, which are invisible by their nature.

The testimony of God consists of three: namely, three witnesses, which are the Father, the Word, and the Holy Spirit. Now, just as a name proceeds from a word, so does every testimony of God; it rests only upon the precious and true witness called the *Amen*, who is the beginning of God’s creation (Revelation 3:14). And the testimony of God stands like this: God the Father, the *Word*, the Holy Spirit, and the *Amen*, who is Christ.

Now, because no one can testify about God except God himself, and since all testimonies of truth must be confirmed by three witnesses, God also testifies about himself through three. However, these are not three persons, but only one single person and one single God; just as in one earthly man, the body, soul, and spirit cannot be three distinct persons, so there are not three persons in God.

<summary>This page argues for the unity of God's essence, asserting that God is one single person rather than three distinct persons, using the analogy of a human's body, soul, and spirit. It identifies Christ as the "Amen" and the "Word" in whom the entire fullness of God resides.</summary>

### Chapter 3. Of the first Principle of all things, etc.

But this is the testimony of God to us in the *Name*—not in Names, but in the Name <note>original: "non in Nominibus, sed in Nomine"</note>—of the *Father, Son,* and *Holy Spirit*. These consist in the Word, and these **three** are one and the same. However, no one acknowledges this testimony except the person who has it within themselves in essence <note>original: "Essentially" — meaning having the actual substance or nature of God within oneself, rather than just an intellectual understanding</note>; that is, the person who has the Spirit from God and is anointed and sealed with it. This is the testimony of God with one word, through which we are sons and heirs of God (Romans 8).

Lastly, the *revelation* of God consists of *seven powers*, which are the *seven spirits of God* (Revelation 1:4, 4:5, 5:6; Zechariah 4:2, 10). These spirits rest upon him who is called and is *Jesse* <note>original: "Jesse" — likely a reference to the "Root of Jesse" mentioned in Isaiah 11, identifying Christ as the fulfillment of this prophecy</note>. He comes from no other, but is self-subsisting <note>term: "self-subsisting" — existing independently by his own nature without depending on anything else</note> through and in himself. In him is everything, and he possesses all things, including the fullness of the Godhead (Revelation 3:1; Isaiah 11; Colossians 2:9). For through him all things were created in Heaven and on Earth, and by him all things are preserved. By him also, all things are redeemed and reconciled. He reigns over all and has everything under his power. Through him, all lost things are restored at his glorious coming. He also holds judgment over all flesh—over the living and the dead <note>original: "quick and dead"</note>. And lastly, he will make old things new, and he will cast away all old things everlastingly.

Therefore, the mystery of God consists in One, Three, and Seven. According to this mystery, all other things are created and exist in One, out of Three, and through Seven. Within that structure, all things are witnessed, understood, revealed, or justified, with absolutely nothing excepted. This is for the reason that God, in his mystery, may be understood and glorified in and through all his creatures.

It identifies Christ as the self-subsisting "Jesse" in whom the fullness of the Godhead dwells and through whom all things are redeemed.</summary>

Through these, all things are numbered, measured, and weighed so perfectly that nothing can be added to them or taken away from them. For all the works of God are perfect and testify to the Creator according to the Mystery of Wisdom; namely, that through His works we may know Him who made them, and understand what and who He is in His Mystery.

# CHAPTER 4

## Concerning the Second Principle, namely <note>original: "viz." (videlicet), a Latin term meaning "that is to say" or "namely"</note> Nature.

**N**ature is the second principle and origin of all things. It stands between God and the elements, through which God works upon the elements as a medium and a means. In its essence, Nature is even like the angelic realm; its origin is a breath, wind, and air blown forth from the Almighty, in which the soul and life of all created things exist. Every living soul is concentrated and joined together essentially, physically, and as a self-subsisting entity in the Tree of Life <note>The "Tree of Life" here likely refers to a mystical or alchemical concept of the source of all vitality within the divine order.</note>, just as God is in Christ and the entire elemental world is within humanity.

This second principle is not everlasting in its beginning, yet it is eternal in its end, just as the angels are. It was not created out of nothing, as this world was; instead, it proceeds from God, just as life comes from the spirit, or as a breath, wind, or air proceeds. It is also the breath of God’s Word <note>The "Word" refers to the *Logos*, the creative power of God often identified with Christ in Christian mysticism.</note>, in which there is life; thus, the speaking of the Word is a living, eternal breath. It is distinct from God in the same way that a living breath or soul is distinct from the spirit that gives it life.

The living breath, soul, or life of all things originates from Nature, but the Spirit

<meta>catchword: Spirit</meta>

<summary>This page defines "Nature" as a divine, intermediate principle that acts as the "breath" of God and the source of life for all things. It describes Nature as an eternal force originating from the Word of God, serving as the bridge between the Creator and the physical elements.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Spirit" mentioned at the end of the previous page, further explaining the distinction between the Spirit, the Soul, and the Body.</meta>

Spirit comes from God, specifically according to its proportion <note>original: "measure"</note>, and the body comes from the Elements. The Spirit, the Soul, and the Life are distinct in this way: God is Eternal Life and the life-giving <note>original: "quickening"</note> Spirit himself; he receives life from no one else because he is the Spirit itself. In contrast, the living soul does not have its life from, by, or through itself, but from the Spirit that makes things alive, whose breath is life. Now, that which is "soulish" <note>This term refers to the *anima* or vital life force, which is distinct from the higher, eternal Spirit.</note> does not have its life from itself, but from the Spirit; it is not a Spirit, but only a breath.

Everything in existence receives the food of its soul and life from Nature. This comes from Heaven through the wind and air, from which everything that has breath lives and feeds, as if through the proceeding breath of the **Word** contained in the second principle. For the **Word** of God feeds every spirit, life, and body with its breath or blowing; life is in the **Word**, which sustains all things by its power, just as it created all things.

Now, as all things consist of Body, Soul, and Spirit, they have three kinds of food for their life and substance:
1. **The bodily food** for the body comes from the Elements, specifically from what comes out of the waters and the earth, which is also where the body originates and is made. 
2. **The soulish food** for the Soul and Life in everything comes from Nature, through the elements of wind and air, from which the life and soul also originate. 
3. **The spiritual food** for the Spirit comes from God, who is the source of the Spirit. Each spirit receives this according to its proportion. This food comes to the Spirit from Heaven in every way, through the Spirit and Light, as if from the three spiritual elements <note>In mystical philosophy, these "spiritual elements" are the divine archetypes or heavenly versions of the physical elements.</note> from which the Spirit also came.

# Chapter 4: Of the second Principle, namely, *Nature*.

Nature assembles itself in its spirit, life, and body into the wind, air, and water.

The angelic world in its body is not earth, as the *elementary* world is, but it is the true body of water from which it exists. That body is ice here below with us, but above it is an angelic earth like a *crystal*. In a word, it is a most noble *salt* of life <note>In this context, "salt" refers to the crystalline, structural principle of spiritual matter, representing stability and permanence.</note>—fertile, constant, and firm over everything—and it is Paradise itself. It is an angelic air, where one does not draw breath like a living *soul*, for the life of nature is *eternal* in its outcome; instead, it lives and moves in the power <note>original: "virtue"</note> of God’s word eternally, without breathing <note>original: *sine respiratione*</note>.

Therefore, death cannot reign in the angelic world over nature or over the Tree of Life, but is instead overcome by it (and even more so by God), for the Tree of Life stands immovable. Therefore, by the breaking of the fruit of this tree at the glorious coming of Christ, everyone shall come from death to life and shall be freed and redeemed from death, the Devil, and the Curse. Finally, in its spirit, it is also an angelic spirit by the power of the word and testimony of God. *Specifically*, this means that the Dragon has no power over it but is conquered by its spirit, cast out, and completely expelled and thrown away—and even more so by God. Therefore, since nature in its spirit is the wind of the Almighty and an outpouring of the light in which God dwells, it cannot approach the evil one, nor can it fall into evil, nor can it be blasted or poisoned by the breath of the ancient *serpent*.

The divine world in its essence <note>original: "being"</note> is compared to the most noble body of water and earth, like a heavenly body which is an *essential* spiritual salt, like the most noble and pure gems, precious stones...

<summary>This page describes the "Angelical world" as a crystalline, eternal realm where life is sustained by God's word rather than physical breathing. It contrasts this stable, holy environment with our "elementary" world, explaining that nature in its highest form is immune to the corruption of the Devil.</summary>

...stones and glistening gold. In its life, it is the breath of the Almighty—a soul and life proceeding out of the mouth of God into and toward an *eternal life*. In its *spirit*, it is the *Spirit* of the Lord itself, who is God, praised forever. God is the Spirit, Nature is the soul or the life, and the Elements are the *body*. But be it known that each world has its own proper *nature* and *element*, and that one world is never changed into another; neither can it be altered, nor can one general principle be turned into another. Now, each principle has its own proper *spirit*, *life*, and *body*.

### Chapter 5.

### *Of the third general Principle, namely the Element.*

God himself is *all in all*. Out of him are all things according to the Spirit; by him are all things according to the word; and to him are all things according to providence or confidence <note>original: "Confidence"; in this theological context, it refers to a state of absolute trust or reliance on divine order.</note>. Romans 11:36; Wisdom 1:7; Chapter 12:1; Genesis 1; John 1; Psalm 104:27–28; 136:25; 145:15–16.

Nature is *everything*, but not in everything; because she is not in God, who has his own nature. The elements also are not *everything*, but are only something—which is a salt <note>As in the previous chapter, "salt" refers to the crystalline, structural principle of matter that gives form and stability to physical things.</note>. This "something" comes from God according to the spirit, from nature according to the life and soul, and from...

# Chapter 5. Of the third general Principle,

...the spiritual water according to the body. And again, the water comes out of the salt; each world is, and flows out of the others.

Nature relates to the angelic world and is an outflowing of the divine world; and the elementary world is an overflowing of nature and the angelic world.

Lastly, man comes forth out of the three worlds and is the concentrated or joined center <note>original: *Centrum*</note> of all the worlds.

There are seven elements or powers of the world: spirit, wind, air, water, light, heaven, and earth. These are the means by which, and through which, this world exists and remains; without them, it cannot survive.

These seven elements are created out of a single substance which, in the elementary world, is "all in all" and is incorruptible; namely salt, which is an excretion of nature—a "residue," as it were, used in a moderate sense <note>original Latin: *Excrementum quasi sobriè sumptum*. In alchemical thought, "excrement" refers to the physical residue or "salt" left behind after a spiritual or natural process.</note>—by which all physical things in this world exist and are preserved. Now, there is a threefold salt: namely, a spiritual salt, a salt of the soul, and a physical and tangible salt. 

The physical salt is fixed and permanent, both in water and fire. From this, we know the substance out of which, and by which, all things stand firm and constant—in both water and fire—so that they may not be destroyed <note>original: "drowned"</note>, and the substance with which they are enclosed. 

The salt of the soul is volatile <note>original: "flying." In alchemy, "flying" refers to substances that evaporate or change state easily, representing the more active, spiritual qualities of life.</note> because life and soul are within it; it is the source of growth by which all things receive both body and life. But when it descends again and turns back into fixed salt, then they receive life. 

But the spiritual salt is a true essence and is the most noble "Being of all Beings" in this world—the spirit of the universe <note>original Latin: *Spiritus universi*</note>. It is the spirit of the elements, their light, and heaven in its very essence.

->The Elements.<-

The Spiritual Salt dwells in the Spirit, Light, and Heaven, and provides for the body of the Resurrection, being spiritual, from the Spirit, Light, and Heaven.

The *Volans* <note>original Latin: *Volans*, meaning flying or volatile.</note> or volatile Salt dwells in the Wind, Air, Rain, and Dew; this provides for the body after death from the wind and air.

The fixed physical <note>original: corporeal</note> Salt dwells in water and earth, from which this body of ours subsists. But Salt is the true fixed Salt and the true water of Life—which is a "dry water"—being both water and Earth together. Within it, the air and wind are secretly hidden, as are the Heavens, Light, and Spirit in its depths. These are the seven Powers of the Element and the world; all seven may easily, undeniably, and clearly be demonstrated to the eye if the substance is analyzed <note>original: "Anatomized," meaning to dissect or chemically break down into component parts.</note>.

This single Element of all Elements is a Power above all powers in this world. Salt is a residue <note>original: "Excrement." In alchemical philosophy, the "excrement" of nature refers to the solid "salt" or physical matter left behind after a spiritual or natural process.</note> of Nature produced by the Word of God; it is the physical seed of the water and of all Elements from which the water sprang, proceeded, or flowed. It was produced by the breathing of the Spirit of the Lord to be a seed for the whole world and was abundantly increased by the movement of the Spirit of the Lord, so that the entire Earth is formed out of it.

<margin>X</margin>
The fixed Salt is threefold: existing in the Earth, in the Water, and in Heaven. The volatile <note>original: "flying"</note> Salt is also threefold: in Rain, Water and Dew, and in Air and Wind. The Essential Salt is also threefold: in the light Spirit and the "upper water." The waters occupy three places or degrees, for the world was established from them—it is remarkable that one is always hidden within the other. The volatile Salt is the Key that opens the way, descending in the Spring so that everything rises from the dead,
